Boyd Group (BGSI) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Self-pay repairs are outpacing insurance volume growth at Boyd.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Boyd Group Services Inc. (BGSI -11.98%) reported third quarter results featuring margin expansion driven by the Project 360 cost-reduction initiative and synergies from recent acquisitions. Management indicated that integration efforts for Joe Hudson’s locations shifted toward embedding the company’s customer-centric operating model and regional incentive structures. The company continued to outperform industry repair volumes while navigating a stable claims environment and improving trends in the total cost of repair. Strategic priorities focused on building market density through a disciplined combination of acquisitions and new start-up locations, supported by a newly launched share repurchase program.

Operator: Good morning everyone. Welcome to the Boyd Group Services, Inc. 2026 Third Quarter Results Conference Call. Listeners are reminded that certain matters discussed in today's conference call or answers that may be given to questions asked could constitute forward-looking statements that are subject to risks and uncertainties relating to Boyd's future financial or business performance. Actual results could differ materially from those anticipated in these forward-looking statements. The risk factors that may affect results are detailed in Boyd's annual information form and other periodic filings and registration statements, and you can access these documents at SEDAR's database found at sedarplus.ca and EDGAR at www.sec.gov. Boyd released its 2026 third quarter results before markets opened today.

You can access the news release as well as the complete financial statements and management discussion and analysis on the company's website at boydgroup.com. The news release, financial statements and MD&A have also been filed on SEDAR+ and EDGAR this morning. On today's call, Boyd will discuss the financial results for the quarter ended September 30, 2026, and provide a general business update. We will then open the call for questions. I'd like to remind everyone that this conference call is being recorded today, Wednesday, November 11, 2026. I would now like to introduce Mr. Brian Kaner, President and Chief Executive Officer of Boyd Group Services, Inc. Please go ahead, Mr. Kaner.

Brian Kaner: Thank you, operator. Good morning, everyone, and thank you for joining us for today's call. On the call with me today is Jeff Murray, our Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer; and Steven Savard, Vice President, Investor Relations and Capital Markets. During the third quarter, we continued to make solid progress across our business. We expanded margins through the disciplined execution of Project 360 and the realization of acquisition synergies while continuing to outperform industry volumes and generate positive same-store sales growth, supported by a sequential improvement in total cost of repair.

We also launched a normal course issuer bid, reinforcing our commitment to a disciplined and balanced approach to capital allocation with a focus on supporting our existing operations, strengthening our market position and creating long-term shareholder value. Revenue increased 28% compared to the third quarter of 2025, while adjusted EBITDA grew 34%, with adjusted EBITDA margins expanding to 13% from 12.4% last year. While I'm pleased with the margin improvement we've achieved this quarter, it's important to take a step back and look at the progress we've made since launching Project 360 in the fourth quarter of 2024.

Since that time, we've improved adjusted EBITDA margins by 230 basis points, reaching 13% this quarter compared to 10.7% in the third quarter of 2024. This progress reflects the benefits of several initiatives we implemented over the past 2 years. In 2025, we introduced our indirect staffing model and made significant progress in bringing scanning and calibration services in-house. We continued building on that foundation in 2026, generating additional savings through indirect procurement and realized synergies from the Joe Hudson's acquisition ahead of schedule. When we launched Project 360, we set a goal of delivering $100 million in cost savings between 2025 and 2029.

With the addition of approximately $40 million in expected synergies from the Joe Hudson's acquisition, which closed in January, that target increased to $140 million. Through the end of the third quarter, we've realized $90 million in savings with another $15 million expected in the fourth quarter, bringing the total to an expected $105 million by year-end. Looking ahead, we expect to capture the remaining $35 million between 2027 and 2029 with a growing emphasis on using technology and AI to improve shop productivity, streamline operations and drive further efficiencies across the business. Turning to Joe Hudson's, as I discussed last quarter, we completed the conversion of its locations on to Boyd's system in an accelerated manner.

We now have greater visibility into the business' operational performance. Our focus has now shifted to embedding Boyd's customer-centric operating model, including our successful WOW Operating Way and our regional management incentive structure across the Joe Hudson's locations. The sequential revenue trend versus the prior year improved during the third quarter with positive momentum continuing into October as the locations progressed under Boyd's operating model. These results support our confidence in their continued improvement and contribution to Boyd's long-term growth and profitability. Turning to industry conditions. Third quarter claims data continues to point to a more stable operating environment with repairable claims volumes estimated to be down 1% to 3% year-over-year.

As we've seen throughout 2026, industry conditions have improved compared to last year and remain broadly consistent with our long-term planning assumptions. We continued to outperform the broader market in the third quarter, generating 2.2% same-store sales growth, driven by higher volumes and modest improvement in total cost of repair. Our continued market share gains reflect the strength of our insurer relationships, improving carrier performance and broad regional market coverage. In October 2026, same-store sales remained positive with continued volume growth supported by a sequential increase in total cost of repair. As we've discussed previously, monthly performance can fluctuate, so we remain focused on underlying trends over the longer periods rather than any individual month's results.

I'll now pass the call over to Jeff, who will provide a more detailed analysis of our third quarter results. Jeff?

Jeff Murray: Thanks, Brian. During the third quarter, sales increased 28% year-over-year to just over $1 billion, driven by $206 million of incremental sales from 342 new locations not in operation for the full prior year period and 2.2% same-store sales growth as we continue to successfully outperform industry volumes during the third quarter. During the quarter, Joe Hudson's locations contributed $170 million in total sales. Gross profit increased to $473 million in the third quarter, up 29% year-over-year, and gross margin increased 40 basis points to 46.7% from 46.3% in the third quarter of 2025.

The expansion in margins was driven by higher paint margins, from acquisition synergies and Project 360 cost savings as well as increased scanning, calibration and sublet margins. This was partially offset by slightly lower labor and parts margins. Turning to operating expenses. In the third quarter, operating expenses as a percentage of sales improved to 33.7% compared to 33.9% in the prior year. This improvement reflects acquisition synergies, cost savings from Project 360 and increased operating leverage from same-store sales growth. If we look back to the third quarter of 2024, just before we launched Project 360, operating expenses were 35% of sales.

Fast forward 2 years and we've improved this ratio by 130 basis points, reflecting the success of Project 360 and more recently, the additional benefits from acquisition synergies. Adjusted EBITDA increased 34% to $132 million over the third quarter of 2025, while adjusted EBITDA margins expanded to 13% from 12.4% in the prior year period. During the third quarter, we realized approximately $15 million in combined synergies from Joe Hudson's and cost savings from Project 360, bringing the year-to-date total to $50 million. Adjusted net earnings in the third quarter was $19 million, up from $17.8 million in the third quarter of 2025 as higher adjusted EBITDA was partially offset by higher depreciation expense and increased finance costs.

Adjusted earnings per share was $0.68 in the quarter compared to $0.83 in the prior year period. For the 9-month period ended September 30, 2026, adjusted earnings per share was $2.06 compared to $1.85 in the same period last year. The company continues to expect maintenance capital expenditures to range between 1.6% and 1.8% of sales for the full year in 2026. Additionally, capital expenditures associated with the Joe Hudson's acquisition remain on track at an estimated $30 million. As Brian mentioned, we launched a normal course issuer bid in September, providing us with additional flexibility in our capital allocation strategy. During the quarter, we repurchased approximately $14 million of our shares, and we also declared our third quarter dividend.

As at September 30, 2026, our pro forma net leverage ratio was 2.9x compared to 3.1x at the end of 2025. I will now pass it back to Brian for closing comments.

Brian Kaner: Thanks, Jeff. As we approach the end of 2026, and I reflect on the year, I'm proud of what our team has accomplished. It has been a busy and productive year for Boyd as we successfully integrated Joe Hudson's, realized meaningful synergies and continue to make progress on Project 360. Importantly, throughout the year, we've maintained our focus on what has been core to Boyd's success for many years: delivering a strong customer experience, improving carrier performance and building lasting relationships with our insurance partners. This focus has been a key driver of our ability to consistently outperform the broader industry and will remain central to our strategy going forward.

With that, I'd now like to open the call to questions. Operator?

Operator: [Operator Instructions] Your first question comes from the line of Sabahat Khan with RBC Capital Markets.

Sabahat Khan: Maybe just for the first question, wanted to get a bit more color, Brian, on the commentary you made about TCOR. It sounds like over the last few quarters, the claims volumes are in a reasonable place. There's likely some market share capture and maybe TCOR and pricing might be the delta versus going higher on the same-store sales side. Can you just maybe give us an update on where TCOR is, pricing discussions with your insurance partners, how those are looking?

Brian Kaner: Yes, yes. So on TCOR, look, we have seen now even leading into October, as we said in the prepared remarks that TCOR continues to improve. We've said for quite some time that the underlying metrics beyond TCOR -- behind TCOR were still moving as they normally moved. We still saw normal pricing increases on the labor side. We still saw a normal price movement on parts through the inflationary environment that we're in. And what had been muting that was just the year-over-year impact of more total losses so bigger tickets coming out of the repair set driving down or mixing down the overall TCOR.

We saw that level out in Q3, and we continue to see it now stable as we get into the fourth quarter. And that's giving us now -- that's allowing the underlying movement in price to actually illuminate. I mean, obviously, the other thing that is driving price up is the increase in calibration services. We continue to see a higher penetration rate of calibration services on the average ticket that we're seeing. And all of those things are starting to move the ticket back in a more positive direction, still muted from the -- I would say, still muted from the, call it, 4% that we expect, but certainly moving its way back towards them.

Sabahat Khan: Great. And then maybe just an update on sort of the start-ups. I think you might have had a few planned for this quarter. I might have got that wrong, but I don't think those happen. Maybe just give us an update on the infrastructure you set up on the new store openings? How many are expected through late this year and maybe the cadence into sort of what could we expect annually going forward?

Brian Kaner: Yes. As you saw in the MD&A, I think we flagged, we've got 34 new locations -- new start-up locations planned for the next 12 months. Like any construction project, we'll see delays where things shift slightly out -- in and out of quarters. You saw after the quarter -- subsequent to the quarter, we announced 7 new locations opening subsequent to the quarter after 10 in the quarter, not just start-up, but all acquisitions. So I think you'll -- I would suggest that we'll get to that kind of 30 to 35, maybe pushing 40-some years of startup locations, which is in line with what we said back in the 5-year -- when we launched our 5-year plan.

Operator: Your next question comes from the line of Chris Murray with ATB Capital Markets.

Chris Murray: Maybe just turning to some of the margin performance and just kind of the cadence as we're sort of walking into, I guess, the target or getting closer to it. Can you just talk about how you're seeing kind of the entire system now behaving with the -- with margins? And your confidence level of being able to get to, I guess, the targets that you set? And how we should think about kind of margin cadence and the pace of improvement as we go into next year?

Brian Kaner: Yes. Well, look, as it relates to the pace of improvement, we said that the balance of the Project 360 savings would come in ratably. So the pace of improvement should be thought of as the incremental savings that are yet to be achieved on Project 360 coming through ratably. That should be the pace of change. Obviously, some incremental same-store sales will help give us a little bit more leverage and push that up a little bit more. But I'm actually very pleased with the cadence that we've been on.

As I said in my opening remarks, we've seen a 230-basis-point improvement from the time we started this project at the end of, call it, Q3 of 2024 to now what we just reported in Q3 of 2026. So the progress on the actual project itself has been very much so in line with what we've expected. The pace, as I said, will be more tempered as you go forward.

And so I think if you look at -- and I think just commenting more broadly on Q3's sequential decline in EBITDA rate, we normally see a 50- to 60-basis-point movement in -- downward movement in Q2 versus -- or Q3 versus Q2 just based on some of the seasonality related to our glass business in particular. So if you think about that, if you would have normally seen 60 basis points of downward pressure, or 50 basis points of downward pressure, we offset that with Project 360 savings.

And we did have some incremental costs in the quarter that related to things like fuel costs and utility costs that were a little bit elevated versus a year ago that ate up some of that savings. But by and large -- and then we had a little bit of leverage loss. So I think -- but by and large, I'm very pleased with where we're at from the progress against the 14% goal that we set in February.

Chris Murray: Okay. That's helpful. And then just one question on acquisition and single-store acquisitions. So we talked a little bit about start-up locations and understandably construction will throw those schedules around. But at the pace that you're kind of running through single-store acquisitions right now, just trying to think of how is the environment out there? I know that there's been -- the midterm sometimes can throw things around and the -- with sellers trying to figure out how to optimize their sales pricing like that, are you seeing any change in the market?

Or is the pace that you're kind of adding stores like single-store operators right now, is that kind of the way we should be thinking about, call it, 6 to 8 a quarter type of thing is a good cadence to be thinking about?

Brian Kaner: Yes. Look, I think we've said this before. I mean, the fourth quarter probably tends to be our largest quarter from an acquisition perspective and store openings. So I wouldn't -- I frankly wouldn't expect anything different this year. I will say that because we are taking a more measured approach to acquisitions, i.e., we're focusing on building density, getting in that #1 or #2 position in the markets that we operate in, that shrinks the pool of available opportunities that are out there for us. It doesn't mean that there's not a lot of available opportunities.

It just means that we're looking for the right opportunity that, again, has the right size box, has -- gives us the ability to do $3.5 million to $4 million of volume out of the location. And when we find that, we're happy to go -- we're happy to engage and purchase. If we don't find that, then we're going to be more apt to look for brownfields and greenfield opportunities to build in those markets. So it just -- it really is just -- there's nothing behind it right now.

The sellers in the marketplace outside of the bigger -- certainly, the regional MSOs and some of the large players that are out there, I think given where we're trading, have to start to see their multiples contract -- their multiple expectations contract. We're not a buyer in that environment, but the opportunity for single shops is still out there.

Mark Jordan: Can you talk about how same-store sales trended throughout the quarter? And maybe what you're seeing quarter-to-date? And I know you said it was positive in October, but just any color as it relates to maybe the rate of change?

Brian Kaner: Yes. I think what we said in the opening remarks is what I would reflect on again. We said that the volume continues to be positive, and we're starting to see some incremental TCOR. So those 2 things at least point us to an environment that we're positive. We're moving away from -- we're evolving our messaging away from giving a pointed amount because we want people to focus on the underlying business performance. We're not providing additional quantitative details. We'll highlight -- we highlighted during the month of October we saw continued volume growth. Once again, we outperformed the industry as well as that sequential improvement in TCOR.

I think that gives you -- that should give -- it should give us the sense that the marketplace continues to be positive.

Mark Jordan: Perfect. And just 1 follow-up. As we think about the parts mix between aftermarket and OEM, how that might have impacted margins for the quarter? Can you just talk about that a little bit because I know it was noted that parts margins were lower year-over-year.

Jeff Murray: Sure. Well, I mean we have seen just really -- there's been some shifting, I think, in the marketplace. And so there's really not much to say. I'd say that I would focus more on the -- I mean, there's been some pretty positive improvements year-over-year in terms of gross margin. There's a lot of factors that were positive. There were a couple that were slightly negative. And we mentioned the parts and labor as being a couple of them, but they really weren't that meaningful. And so some element of it was aftermarket parts related. But again, I would focus more on the net positive overall.

Brian Kaner: Yes. And I'd just follow on to that, that the -- typically, on a quarter-over-quarter basis, Q2 to Q3, the erosion that I talked about in the margin, the EBITDA margin, most of that falls in gross margin. So if you just think about the sequential margin decline, we did report a couple of tens of points of benefit on a year-over-year basis, but the sequential margin decline is simply more tied to that seasonality and the seasonality is more tied to the -- just the reductions we see in the glass business as we get into the third quarter.

Operator: Your next question comes from the line of Steven Hansen with Raymond James.

Steven Hansen: Brian or Jeff, I just wanted to ask you about your conviction in the buyback around that opportunity that you see there. Valuations obviously come down a fair bit. Brian, you just referenced that it's more expensive to buy the multi-shop MSOs now, single shops may be still doable. But how do you feel about the pace of the buyback relative to some of the opportunities for acquisitions out there in the market?

Brian Kaner: Yes. Look, I mean, as our continue to -- as our business evolves, I mean, so does our capital allocation opportunities. That includes the addition of share repurchases. The focus of the business remains on directing capital towards the opportunities that frankly offer the most attractive risk-adjusted returns and create long-term shareholder value. We're not going to be prescriptive about what we're doing on share buybacks. I think we'll -- we have that as an option in our capital allocation strategy now. And if there aren't other places to deploy capital, that does become a -- that does certainly become an option for us.

Steven Hansen: Okay. That's helpful. And then just lastly, I just wanted to go back to just the M&A environment just quickly and how you think the -- some of the smaller shop sellers, I think it sounds like the multiple -- multiple opportunity there is still quite good. The MSOs in the sort of the smaller tier MSOs are still probably off the table. But does the flywheel allow you to continue to move fast enough to meet your targets? Or do you feel like you can slow a little bit and focus on the greenfield opportunity a little more, I don't know, aggressively if that's the right word?

But -- and how do you feel about that MSO -- single-shop MSO versus the greenfield opportunity?

Brian Kaner: Yes. I mean, look, I think the desire to -- the acquisition strategy is still -- the whole new unit growth strategy is still predicated off of building out market density. And so as we've talked about before, we have a map that has dots on the map and our acquisition team is out in the marketplace looking for the opportunities to fill that dot on the map. And so it seems -- it comes across as a little bit more opportunistic, where we'll go into the marketplace. It's just the pacing of the way that the team can get into the market to fill those opportunities.

So if you get into a market and you realize that there's not somebody to buy in the market, now you've got to get somebody that's out looking for land in the market or looking for a brownfield opportunity in that market. So I think it's because we've gotten a lot more purposeful with our capital deployment and have just gotten very specific around where we want to grow, that inherently will slow the pace. If I had a shotgun approach to buying across the United States, there's all kinds of places. I'm sure there's places I could buy in Wyoming and Idaho and Montana and places that I ultimately don't want to be.

But the opportunity is to go build out densification just narrows the pool of things that we can go do and places that we can go buy. And that, I think, puts -- it puts a little bit of pressure on the pace, but that's okay because it's ultimately driving -- we know that the success of those -- we're hedging our bets on success on the ones that we are buying.

Jeff Murray: And Brian, I'll just add to that because we're opportunistic it has -- it is lumpy. If you look back over time, as a result, it's has been lumpy over time, and we'll continue to have some level of lumpiness. The start-up strategy does mitigate that to some degree because you're going to have some bleeding in that are essentially planned for like that. But otherwise, there's still lumpiness.

Brian Kaner: Yes. And look, we said in the MD&A, we've got 34 new start-up locations. That's the -- those are the ones that we have certainty around and acquisitions will, as Jeff said, they'll just be lumpy.

Operator: Your next question comes from the line of Derek Lessard with TD Cowen.

Derek Lessard: I just wanted to maybe just hit on the volume trends in the quarter. It was down 1% to 3%, but that seems like it was a bit of a deterioration compared to Q2. I think it was down 0% to 2%. So maybe just talk about some of the trends that you were seeing there? And then maybe specific to you guys, looks like you did outperform the industry a little bit better in Q3. So if there's anything you'd like to point out there?

Brian Kaner: Yes. And you're right. The volume in the quarter did contract a bit, a little bit. I think a large portion of that is, if you look at vehicle miles traveled in the quarter, you saw some suppression in vehicle miles traveled in the summer months. That's largely what probably drove the decline in volume, still within the range that we expected to be. So it's not that it's -- it didn't go back down to the high single digits, it just -- it stayed in that band. Relative to our performance against it, remember, we said there was a little bit more TCOR movement in the quarter.

So that gave us a little bit more -- a little bit of incremental spread against what the -- how the market was performing. We talk about claims in terms of units. We talk about same-store sales in terms of dollars. So that incremental TCOR helped us increase the spread, albeit the volume spread, if you will, is probably fairly consistent.

Jeff Murray: I think it's also worth pointing out, it's the third quarter in a row now where we're essentially in the typical range of 0% to [ 3% ] so...

Derek Lessard: Absolutely. And then maybe 1 last 1 for me. Just correct me if I'm wrong. I think you guys are pretty much done on the internalization of your scanning and calibration. Are you able to quantify the margin impact on that $90 million year-to-date realized? Or maybe just highlight sort of like the biggest margin bucket contributors to that -- to the $90 million in savings you've realized so far?

Jeff Murray: I think you're talking about the -- just the overall progress on both the Project 360 and the synergies from the Joe Hudson's transaction?

Derek Lessard: Right. And I guess the -- I guess, what did the scanning and calibration contribute to those savings that you realized?

Brian Kaner: Yes. I don't know that -- we haven't split out the incremental improvement we're getting from scanning and calibration. That is -- I think I'll answer your first part of your question. We are at the threshold that we said we would be at. We're at and beyond, frankly, the 80% coverage of internalization. That team needs to continue to build as you see higher penetration rates of calibration services now. So it isn't that the journey is done because now we need to continue to add staff to maintain the growing environment that we're in. I just want to -- just to make sure we're clear.

I mean, the $90 million that you're referencing is we said we'd be at $105 million by the end of the year on total Project 360 savings, not a year-to-date, but a project-to-date is the $90 million. Jeff Murray: And maybe just add to -- for clarity that the internalization of scanning and calibration was not part of those numbers. However, there was some element of synergy element.

Brian Kaner: Yes. Brian Kaner: Yes. Publisher Grindstone and developer Games Farm have announced Conan: The Black Pyramid, a roguelite action RPG “where sword and sworcery meets cosmic horror.” It supports two-player online cooperative play, and will launch for PC via Steam in 2027.

Here is an overview of the game, via its Steam page:

The Black Pyramid calls to warriors across time. Conan has answered.

Conan: The Black Pyramid is an action roguelite RPG where sword and sorcery meets cosmic horror. Fight alone or with a friend in two-player online cooperative play. Shape your build with every run, turn chance to your advantage, and return with new ways to challenge the darkness.

Explore the distant lands of Robert E. Howard’s shared universe and take control of such iconic heroes as Conan, Solomon Kane, Dark Agnes, and more. Descend into eldritch chambers of the Black Pyramid, a mysterious and dangerous place that defies the logic of space and time. Confront the shadows of the champions themselves and piece together the dark secrets of their past.

Choose your own path through the shifting labyrinth of ancient ruins, ravaged kingdoms and occult halls ripped from different eras of time. Slay nightmarish monsters, savage beasts and bloodthirsty fanatics, and face off against challenging bosses that guard every layer of the Black Pyramid.

Every run presents new routes, new combat possibilities and new story events that move you closer to learning the truth: who summoned you here?

Choose from five distinct characters and weave crushing melee blows, precise swordplay, ranged gunfire and magical attacks into powerful combos. Dodge away from danger and stagger enemies with precisely timed parries to unleash brutal executions, or wear them down with dangerous status effects. Switch weapons and relics to adapt to every combat situation and outsmart your opponents.

Discover multiple Warrior Memories for every character, each with their unique combination of weapons and abilities that drastically change the playstyle. Define your build with ancient artifacts that grant new devastating effects, introduce new combat mechanics, or save you from a fatal blow. Combine them with upgradable Combat Masteries to create your own version of the unstoppable champion. Become even stronger with every attempt as you level up your heroes and unlock permanent upgrades with Wakestones.

As you search the Pyramid for perfect items for your build, you collect dice that increase your chances of better loot. Add more dice to your result and unlock additional rewards now, or save them for later when the stakes are higher.

Fight the nightmares of the Pyramid alone or with a friend in 2-player online co-op. Share the journey, earn individual rewards and keep your progression. Experiment with different characters and build combinations to truly dominate the foe, side by side.

Femminile, le formazioni Under 17 e Under 15 partecipano ai campionati maschili Under 14 PRO e Under 13 PRO

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Una nuova sfida e un passo importante nel percorso di formazione delle giovani rossonere. La stagione 2026/27 porta con sé una significativa novità per il Settore Giovanile femminile: le formazioni Under 17 e Under 15 partecipano rispettivamente ai campionati maschili Under 14 PRO e Under 13 PRO, affrontando le squadre giovanili dei club professionistici. Se nella passata stagione le due formazioni si confrontavano con squadre maschili a livello provinciale e dilettantistico, rispettivamente Under 15 e Under 14, oggi la sfida assume una nuova dimensione. Una scelta innovativa che vede il Milan come la prima società italiana a intraprendere questa strada. Nella seconda parte della stagione le rossonere torneranno invece alle competizioni femminili di categoria.

Alla base del progetto c'è una precisa visione, raccontata da Elisabet Spina, Head of Women Football: "La nostra è una sfida culturale che vuole portare ad aumentare la competitività nel calcio femminile". La decisione nasce anche dagli ottimi risultati ottenuti nella passata stagione contro le formazioni dilettantistiche, che hanno spinto il Club a ricercare un livello di confronto più elevato. Da qui la scelta di affrontare squadre di società professionistiche, pur abbassando di un anno l'età degli avversari. Il confronto con ritmi e caratteristiche fisiche differenti permette alle giovani rossonere di mettersi alla prova in situazioni di gioco nuove. "Il Milan è un club professionistico e noi vogliamo offrire le medesime opportunità a tutti", sottolinea Spina.

A raccontare l'esperienza direttamente dal campo è Arianna Raso, sedicenne capitana dell'Under 17. "Nel giocare contro i maschi cambia soprattutto l'intensità, sia dal punto di vista fisico, sia da quello tecnico", spiega, sottolineando come il ritmo elevato e la pressione costante richiedano maggiore rapidità di pensiero e di esecuzione. Le difficoltà diventano così parte integrante del percorso: dalla gestione dei duelli fisici alla necessità di mantenere un'intensità costante, fino alla capacità di reagire con lucidità alla pressione degli avversari. Un confronto vissuto con competitività e rispetto reciproco, come evidenzia la stessa Arianna: "Alla fine, in campo siamo tutti calciatori che vogliono competere e migliorarsi".

Un progetto che guarda anche al futuro, con l'obiettivo di continuare a sviluppare nuove opportunità per le giovani calciatrici. Il confronto nei campionati maschili rappresenta un primo importante passo di un percorso che potrebbe evolversi ulteriormente, come sottolineato da Spina, che individua negli allenamenti misti una possibile prospettiva per l'attività di base. Una strada ancora da esplorare, ma che conferma la volontà del Club di investire nella formazione e nella crescita del proprio Settore Giovanile femminile.

Nvidia Stock Just Had Its Price Target Slashed 60%. Others Still Say Buy. Who's Right?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Baird downgraded Nvidia on Wednesday and cut its price target to $200. Morgan Stanley still sees the stock reaching $300, and others also remain bullish. Here's what each side is betting on and what investors should watch next week.

As uncertainty whirls around CEO Jensen Huang's blistering scandal, Wall Street can't agree on what Nvidia (NVDA +4.56%) is worth.

On Wednesday morning, Baird downgraded the chipmaker to Neutral from Outperform and cut its price target by 60%, to $200 from $500. Within hours, Morgan Stanley took Nvidia off its list of top stock ideas but kept its Overweight rating and a $300 target. That leaves two respected firms about $100 a share apart on the world's most important AI company.

The stock shrugged off both calls. Nvidia rose nearly 5% to about $177 by midday Wednesday as semiconductor stocks rallied after strong results from AI cloud company CoreWeave (CRWV +26.41%). Even so, Nvidia is still about 26% below its Oct. 6 record close of $239.24.

Baird's concern isn't today's demand. Its analyst said lead times for Nvidia's newest chips remain stretched, with demand well exceeding supply.

Instead, the worry is next year. Baird said it is pushing back its 2027 data center forecasts due to a recent lack of visibility. Supply problems are the main reason, but the firm also sees possible softening in demand because spending by the big cloud companies could grow by 40% or slightly less in 2027.

Baird also said uncertainty about Huang's tenure supports a Neutral rating. Nvidia's board is investigating whether Huang was the target of a foreign intelligence effort, after he acknowledged an affair with an executive at a company that buys Nvidia hardware. Nvidia's own annual report warns that it is "highly dependent on the services of our long-standing executive team."

Meanwhile, Morgan Stanley's move looks bigger than it is. The firm removed Nvidia from its top picks because of a rule that a top pick that falls more than 25% from the day it was named is automatically dropped. Nvidia was named on Oct. 2.

Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore said there is no change to the firm's view of a strong and durable AI buildout, but he acknowledged that near-term uncertainty will persist as investors debate how crucial Huang is to Nvidia's direction. Wells Fargo also has a $300 price target on Nvidia. The bank gave a $15 trim to the target on Tuesday but said it expects Nvidia to report solid earnings that will help assuage investor concerns about the durability of the AI investment cycle.

The latest numbers back up the bulls. In Nvidia's most recent quarter, revenue rose 106% to $96.2 billion, and data center sales reached $89 billion. The company guided for about $108 billion in revenue this quarter, even assuming no data center computing sales in China.

Look closely, and the two firms aren't that far apart on the business. Both say demand is strong right now. Even Baird's cautious scenario has the largest customers increasing their AI spending by about 40% in 2027, which is a slowdown but still a lot of growth.

The real gap is about how much to discount the stock for a possible supply crunch and the chance that Huang leaves. At about $177, Baird's $200 target implies roughly 13% upside. Morgan Stanley's $300 target implies about 70%.

For long-term investors, the bull case looks better grounded in Nvidia's actual results, but the next week matters. Nvidia reports quarterly earnings on Nov. 17, and investors should listen for a few things: whether its forecast for next quarter holds up, what management says about supply, and whether it addresses the board's investigation.

If the numbers stay strong and the investigation clears Huang, today's price could look cheap in hindsight. But if guidance slips, Baird's caution will look smart. Either way, investors who own Nvidia should keep the position at a size they can live with because the swings in AI stocks surely aren't over.

History Says This Is What Will Happen to Sandisk Stock After Its 8-for-1 Split

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sandisk recently announced an 8-for-1 stock split.

Since separating from Western Digital in February 2025, Sandisk (SNDK +16.49%) has benefited from soaring demand for data storage, particularly from artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure.

However, the company's latest announcement has introduced an interesting twist. On Oct. 29, Sandisk announced an 8-for-1 stock split, scheduled to take effect after the market closes on Nov. 19.

Normally, stock splits generate excitement among investors. But Sandisk stock has actually plunged since the announcement. Between Oct. 29 and Nov. 9, Sandisk fell from $1,330 to $886, a 33% decline.

Let's examine how stock splits work, what happened with other semiconductor companies that split their stock in recent years, and whether Sandisk is worth buying before its upcoming split.

A stock split increases the number of outstanding shares while proportionately reducing their price. For example, an investor holding one Sandisk share worth $800 before the split would subsequently own eight shares worth approximately $100 apiece post-split. The investment would still be worth $800.

As investors can see, stock splits don't create shareholder value, change a company's earnings, or alter its market capitalization. So, why do companies pursue them?

One reason is accessibility. Lower share prices in terms of absolute dollars can make stocks more appealing to retail investors who prefer purchasing whole shares rather than fractional shares. That's precisely what Sandisk wants to accomplish. Management explained that its stock price has appreciated substantially since the Western Digital spinoff, making a split attractive for a broader base of investors and employees participating in the company's stock purchase program.

The announcement accompanied impressive financial results. During its fiscal first quarter of 2027, Sandisk generated $11 billion in revenue, increasing 378% year over year and 23% sequentially. Adjusted earnings per share reached $49.51 per share compared with just $1.22 during the same period last year.

These results reflect robust demand for NAND flash memory, particularly as data centers require more storage capacity. However, Sandisk's post-earnings sell-off suggests investors are becoming more concerned about valuation and the sustainability of memory pricing.

In June 2024, Nvidia (NVDA +4.56%) completed a 10-for-1 split after its shares surged alongside demand for its GPUs. Nvidia subsequently experienced prolonged volatility, including a sharp pullback during the summer of 2024. Nevertheless, its shares eventually reached higher levels as revenue and earnings continued expanding.

Broadcom (AVGO +6.74%) followed with its own 10-for-1 split in July 2024. Similar to Nvidia, Broadcom benefited from growing AI infrastructure spending, particularly demand for custom accelerators and networking technology. Its stock also experienced fleeting fluctuations after the split before benefiting from continued acceleration in its AI semiconductor business.

I wouldn't be surprised to see Sandisk experience a short-term rebound once its shares begin trading at a lower nominal price. Using the Nov. 9 closing price of $886, the theoretical post-split share price would be around $111, assuming no additional price movement. That's considerably more accessible than a stock trading near $900.

The lower price could attract more investors who previously considered Sandisk too expensive to purchase in whole-share increments. However, increased interest from retail investors doesn't necessarily translate into sustainable stock price appreciation.

Here's where I'd exercise caution: Sandisk's recent financial results have been historic, but the memory industry is also cyclical. Demand for data center storage is growing rapidly, while supply constraints are contributing to higher average selling prices. Those conditions support Sandisk's margins and profitability... for now.

Investors need to understand the difference between a stock becoming cheaper per share and becoming cheaper based on valuation. An 8-for-1 split doesn't reduce Sandisk's price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio. If investors bid up shares after the split, they could ironically end up paying an even higher valuation for a business whose profits are sensitive to memory pricing cycles.

Sandisk could enjoy a brief rally around the time of its split, especially after the recent sell-off. However, given what happened with Nvidia and Broadcom, history suggests the momentum will not be sustainable without continued earnings growth.

Nvidia and Broadcom ultimately rewarded shareholders because their underlying businesses grew, not because dividing their shares magically created value. Sandisk could follow a similar path if demand for its NAND flash storage remains strong. For now, though, I think patience is the more prudent strategy.

I'm modding GTA 5 every day in a quest to either transform it into a PC version of GTA 6 or break it entirely. Come back tomorrow to find out what new horrible thing I've done to Rockstar's baby, or check out what I got up to earlier.

As we've already discussed, I regard the personal automobile as a vector of poisonous ideology. Its beneficent alternative? The train: socialised, communal, predictable, subject to the gentle yet firm hand of rational state planning. If the world had more trains in it, we wouldn't be where we are now. We'd be somewhere else. On account of catching the train.

I have converted Grand Theft Auto 5's Franklin Clinton into one of the hero-drivers of these great iron beasts. I have installed Los Santos Metro Driver, which transforms Rockstar's opus from crime sim to train sim. Or tram sim, I guess. Same thing. The city's denizens need to move between airport terminals and only man can help them do it (you are that man).

Did you know driving trains, like, sucks? It's hard. You'd think it'd be easy, what with being essentially glued to your surface of operation, but I have spent most of my career as a trainee train-er overshooting platforms, accidentally applying the emergency brake, and sometimes running over passengers. Which, whatever.

It is an almost alarmingly detailed simulation. You gather driver XP with which you ascend to new heights of train mastery. You are paid a wage. Your pay is docked if you overshoot stations. Crushing customers to paste doesn't seem to affect much.

Also, did you know train magic disrupts teleportation magic? My teleport-every-50-seconds mod remains in effect, the countdown timer glowing ominously on my screen in the midst of everything I do. And yet, somehow, while driving a train it is thwarted. It is a pleasant and newfound freedom, just one of the many positives of the train life.

Quote of the day by former GTA 6 and Red Dead Redemption 2 artist: Sony and Microsoft can’t compete with Rockstar because “they do not care about making game products”

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

And Rockstar's institutional knowledge is "absolutely priceless"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Rockstar's ability to create world-beating titles like GTA 6 stems from a core practice - not succumbing to the 'fire and rehire' cycle that has ravaged publishers like Microsoft and Sony.

That's according to David O'Reilly, a 12-year veteran of Rockstar whose credits as an environment artist cover GTA 6, Red Dead Redemption 2, GTA 5. He claims that "Rockstar doesn't do the whole thing that the games industry has been madly scrambling to do" over the past few years - namely, laying off vast swathes of staff to cut costs, before scaling back up when production needs require doing so.

"How does Rockstar do it?" he asks. "How are they performing these magical feats of game development? It's pretty simple really: They've not engaged in that fire/rehire cycle." The result, O'Reilly claims, is that "Rockstar has kept nearly all of its institutional knowledge," and that in his time at the studio he worked alongside people who had been at Rockstar North when it was still known as DMA Design, a rebrand that took place in early 2002.

That heritage, he says, "is a big deal in today's gaming industry," and the associated "knowledge bank" that comes with keeping your long-serving staff around is invaluable. It's also something that "Sony, Microsoft, all these big publishers [...] don't understand, because they're focused on the money. They don't give a crap about making games. Make no mistake - they do not care about making game products."

That apathy is something O'Reilly believes even Take-Two, Rockstar's long-standing publisher, shares. The difference is that the company recognises "that Rockstar is a Golden Goose," and remains very hands-off with the development studio "because they're terrified of breaking something in that winning formula. [...] They let Rockstar cook their good stuff, and it's worked for a long time and it's continuing to work."

O'Reilly's position - that a short-term approach of firing and rehiring does nothing to actually help a studio - is certainly one that's beginning to be vindicated. After thousands of layoffs and dozens of studio closures across the industry over recent years, the idea we're getting better games as a result is laughable. In fact, the opposite is true - in the past, plenty of classics were made because the teams that made the first game all stuck around to improve the sequel. And just this week, Zelda producer Eiji Aonuma pointed out that the Ocarina of Time remake owes some of its success to the fact Nintendo doesn't engage in mass layoffs.

Perhaps his claim - that "noone is competing with Rockstar" - is not entirely fair. For one thing, there are studios out there that are widely retaining talent and are continuing to push out acclaimed games. For another, Rockstar's resource - fuelled by billions in GTA 5 revenue - is literally world-beating. But maybe you can't really put a figure on it - as O'Reilly says: At Rockstar, you have a lot of people who have been there 20 years or more, [...] and that sort of knowledge is absolutely priceless.

‘Grey’s Anatomy’ Texas Spinoff Casts ‘How to Get Away With Murder’ Alum Karla Souza in Lead Role

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The “Grey’s Anatomy” spinoff set in West Texas has cast Karla Souza in a lead role, Variety has learned.

The spinoff was originally announced at ABC in May. It was intended to launch during the 2026-2027 broadcast season, but was recently delayed to the 2027-2028 season after it did not receive a California TV tax credit.

The official logline for the untitled series describes it as “an edgy drama about a team at a West Texas rural medical center — the last chance for care before miles of nowhere.” Related Stories Jessica Rothe SXSW Sci-Fi Thriller 'Imposters' Sells to Brainstorm Media for U.S., Racks Up International Deals Ahead of AFM (EXCLUSIVE) 'Cheers,' 'Taxi' Showrunner Ken Estin and Mentalist Banachek Take Psychic Fraud Series 'Psychic Predators' From Taiwan Creative Content Fest to American Film Market (EXCLUSIVE)

Souza will star as Dr. Carolina “Lina” Hall, who is described as “a haunted and cynical doctor who runs a medical center in a small town in West Texas.”

The role brings Souza back into the ABC and Shondaland fold. She previously starred in the Emmy-winning ABC series “How to Get Away With Murder” in the role of Laurel Castillo. She also starred in the ABC sitcom “Home Economics,” which aired for three seasons. Souza’s other credits include “Day Shift” at Netflix, the series “56 Days” and the film “La Caida” at Amazon, with Souza winning an International Emmy Award for the film, and the Mexican films “¿Qué culpa tiene el niño?,” “Nosotros los Nobles,” and “Instructions Not Included.”

She is repped by Gersh, Untitled, and Gang Tyre.

Shonda Rhimes and Meg Marinis are the co-creators and executive producers on the spinoff series. “Grey’s Anatomy” star Ellen Pompeo is also an executive producer, as is Betsy Beers. 20th Television and Shondaland produce.

This will now be the third spinoff of “Grey’s Anatomy.” In addition to the mothership show, ABC has also aired the spinoffs “Private Practice” and “Station 19,” which ran for six seasons and seven seasons respectively. ABC also aired the digital series “Grey’s Anatomy: B-Team” on ABC.com in 2018.

Democrats Won. When Do Hollywood Crews Get Back to Work?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tom Curley is happy about the election results. Whether they’ll help him get back to work is another matter. The production sound mixer, who won an Oscar for “Whiplash,” told IndieWire he has struggled to find consistent employment since his last gig, “CSI: Vegas,” was canceled in 2024.

He’s picked up some work here and there, but nothing has restored the professional footing Curley said he had just two years ago: a loss that has felt “humiliating.”

“I kind of feel like I was in a car accident almost, and I’m just here staring at the aftermath, wondering how I’m going to pick up the pieces, if I’m ever going to pick up the pieces,” he said. “It’s a very destabilizing thing.” Related Stories DC Studios’ Latest Reboot: James Gunn and Peter Safran’s Exit Is About Cost-Cutting, Not Just Creative Issues Attend, the First Marketplace Connecting Filmmakers Directly to Theater Owners, Finally Launches Today

Before last week’s midterms, Curley was cautiously optimistic about a proposed federal tax credit designed to keep more film and TV production work inside the U.S. Now, he’s worried the blue wave could inadvertently slow a bill that had been gaining momentum on both sides.

With Republicans meeting such dramatic electoral losses last week, Curley fears Trump could withdraw his support for the proposed tax credit as a kind of punishment.

“All it takes is some hurt feelings to get in the way,” he said.

But the election was mostly good news for Hollywood, right? Democrats won back both chambers of Congress, and Xavier Becerra (also a Democrat) will soon succeed Gavin Newsom as governor of California — still home to the country’s largest film and TV production workforce.

Meanwhile in LA, Nithya Raman beat incumbent Mayor Karen Bass after explicitly campaigning to make it easier to make movies here. Her victory was widely seen as a rebuke of the status quo in a city where production jobs have become increasingly difficult to find. It’s also part of a broader Democratic promise to make the economy work better for the people who actually depend on it, crews included.

Yet for the scads of film and TV workers IndieWire spoke to in Los Angeles, being pleased with the election results politically didn’t necessarily mean expecting more work would come their way.

Hollywood is a state of mind, sure. But it’s also a literal place where residents have rent due even when the industry isn’t thriving. Inflation, gig-economy restrictions, SAG-AFTRA contract uncertainty, permitting headaches, artificial intelligence, inaccessible healthcare, corporate consolidation, and, yes, productions increasingly leaving town are making the gaps between paychecks harder to survive.

Legislation can help crews in L.A., but Hollywood executives still decide what gets made, where it gets made, and who gets hired. For someone like Curley, even one more gig would help with the bills. The harder question is what comes after that opportunity wraps, and whether today’s labor market can still offer the people who make movie magic enough for a long-term living.

The first test for this new establishment comes even before the majority takes office.

In the first quarter of 2025, roughly 46 percent of scripted American projects tracked by Luminate were filmed entirely abroad. That’s part of a broader trend toward international production that has drawn bipartisan concern in the U.S. since the late ‘90s.

Right now, Sen. Adam Schiff and Rep. Laura Friedman, both Democrats from California, are trying to pass a federal production tax credit through the outgoing Congress during the lame-duck session. The bill was introduced in September with bipartisan backing — including from South Carolina Sen. Tim Scott and Texas Rep. Nathaniel Moran, both Republicans returning to Congress next session.

The proposed Motion Picture, Television, and Entertainment Revitalization Act would lower the cost of hiring film and TV workers in the U.S. by giving eligible productions a credit covering 20 percent of qualifying pay. Bonuses could bring that up to 30 percent, although qualifying projects would generally need to cost over $1 million and spend at least 75 percent of their principal photography days in the U.S.

Some domestic post-production and visual effects work would qualify for federal tax breaks, too, and paired with the right state incentives, film producers could reliably use the credits’ savings to motivate new work. Curley said the people who’d hired him before were explicitly waiting for a federal incentive along those lines. That made the proposal feel like a possible job, not just another bill.

“I’ve been in a survival state of mind for the past couple of years, and I would say my nerves are pretty frayed,” he said. “So I’m extra sensitive to potential downfalls to this whole plan that we’re hoping goes through.” Still, legislators say there’s cause for hope.

Rep. Friedman told IndieWire on November 9 that the House bill had 54 cosponsors, evenly split between Democrats and Republicans. “I’m working to get this done as soon as possible because film and television workers need these jobs back now, not in a few months or a couple of years,” she said.

With weeks left in the current Congress, attaching the credit to a larger bill already headed for a vote could speed things up. But responding to IndieWire, Friedman didn’t identify a specific legislative package that could carry the measure to a scheduled floor date right now. If the proposal doesn’t become law before this session ends, lawmakers will have to reintroduce the initiative in 2027.

“It will take both parties working together to get this across the finish line, and we are pushing hard to make this much-needed incentive a reality,” Sen. Schiff told IndieWire in a statement.

As written, Friedman noted the credit could possibly take effect as early as January 1, 2026 — again, if the bill is signed by year’s end. That could establish when production could qualify, but it doesn’t tell us when out-of-work crews might actually start getting calls. What’s more, when those job offers finally do come through? They could be far from old creative hubs like Los Angeles.

Richard Sanchez has a production job in California right now, and he knows how much that counts.

“I’m one of the very lucky folks who is working at the moment,” the visual effects editor told IndieWire by email. “But admittedly it’s lean and has been lean for quite a while.”

Tax credits have put states in fierce competition for film and TV jobs, from the first day of shooting to the final edit. And in September, Gov. Gavin Newsom signed AB 2319 — specifically creating a separate California tax credit for post-production work. The state is scheduled to start allocating those credits on July 1, 2027, and the state budget will ultimately determine how much is available each year.

“It’s a start, it needs more funding but I hope that brings work in,” Sanchez told IndieWire.

California already makes up to $750 million a year available through its broader film and TV production credit. That previously expanded program began in July 2025 and generally covers 35 percent of qualifying spending, with a higher initial rate for TV series that relocate here. That can help California land a series, but it doesn’t guarantee steady employment: Studios still decide how many episodes to order and where to send the post-production work.

In the third quarter of 2026, projects in the program accounted for 40.3 percent of scripted on-location filming in Greater Los Angeles (nearly double their share a year earlier), according to FilmLA’s quarterly report. But over the same period, total on-location filming across the region fell 4.8 percent.

“Where the tax credit applies, it’s working to bring productions back to Los Angeles and put local crews to work,” FilmLA CEO Denise Gutches said in a statement to IndieWire. “Still, overall production remains far below where Los Angeles needs it to be, and crews and businesses across the region are feeling it.”

FilmLA counts shoot days, not jobs or paychecks. So, those figures can’t tell us how many people were hired, how much they earned, or whether they worked enough to qualify for benefits. One person who responded to IndieWire’s Reddit callout and identified themselves as a Bay Area camera operator said they were working on a union production but doubted they’d log enough hours to get health coverage.

Gov.-elect Xavier Becerra called the current program “the floor” during his campaign and pledged to expand. Newsom’s proposal would raise the annual cap to at least $1 billion, make expenses such as actors’ pay eligible, and set aside $100 million a year for indie productions. Those changes still need legislative approval. Becerra’s first budget will give crews a better idea of what he’s prepared to seek.

Curley got a brief stint with the U.S. main unit of “Artificial,” which shot partly in San Francisco. Most of the shoot, he said, took place in Italy. A federal credit could make a U.S. shoot more attractive to producers facing that kind of choice — but then there’s the question of where in the U.S. they’ll go.

Georgia, for example, offers a 20 percent base credit plus another 10 percentage points for productions that promote the state, with no annual cap on how much productions can earn in credits. California and Georgia count different expenses, so the percentages are only part of the calculation.

And a producer weighing L.A. and Atlanta gets to run the numbers both ways — while a crew member who’s spent years building a career in one city has a different calculation to make. If the next show goes across the country, can they afford to follow it? Can they afford to stay put and wait?

“We can’t always be the cheapest, so we need to be the easiest.” That’s how Pamala Buzick Kim, a co-founder of the production advocacy group Stay in LA, sees the city’s job supporting the industry.

A tax credit can make a shoot look good on paper. But someone still has to get the permits, pay the fees, and figure out what happens when the next location runs across a city line. Buzick Kim said mayor-elect Nithya Raman’s office regularly talked with Stay in L.A. about what was getting in the way of filming and what the city could practically do about it. Those conversations made her optimistic.

Raman’s office did not immediately respond to IndieWire’s request for comment. But her campaign platform called for a staffed film office within the mayor’s office, clearer permit timelines, lower fees for smaller productions, and regular reviews of neighborhood filming restrictions. She also pledged to work with the county and neighboring jurisdictions so productions could move more easily.

“If we’re going to incentivize more people to roll here we need to cut down permit processing times, lower fees, and lift unnecessary restrictions on local filmmaking,” echoed film and TV editor Andy Young in an email to IndieWire. Young volunteered for Raman in the primary and worked as a paid freelance video editor for her campaign during the general election. (He said he was speaking for himself.)

Some of Raman’s proposed changes will take cooperation across local groups. For example, FilmLA coordinates applications and charges for its services, but city officials approve permits and set some of the fees that FilmLA ultimately collects. A new administration at City Hall will have to work with the people already making those decisions to successfully enact changes.

For a smaller shoot, every cost makes a difference. FilmLA and the city recently extended their low-impact permit pilot through April 30, 2027, and qualifying productions can apply for just $350, compared with the standard $931 application fee. A few days on a commercial can pay the bills between longer jobs, and Buzick Kim wants city residents to see community when film crews take over their block.

“How do we get residents to be more favorable and optimistic about production?” she asked for Stay in LA. “How do we get them understanding that these are your neighbor’s jobs?”

Curley said he didn’t know enough about permitting to say how much a film office would help L.A. For years, he said, the city’s reputation as the best place in the world to make a movie “was our film office.” But together, his dry spell and the Stay in LA pitch now assume that physical Hollywood, like anywhere else, has to earn its place in a competitive national conversation.

California voters chose new leadership in Washington, the state capital of Sacramento, and LA’s City Hall. They didn’t get a vote on who would own two of Hollywood’s biggest studios.

On October 6, less than a month before the election, Paramount Skydance completed its acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery. The deal followed a separate earlier merger in August 2025, when Skydance and Paramount Global combined.

In its November 9 investor update, Skydance said it was aiming for at least $6 billion in annual savings within three years. Those plans include combining back-office functions and using more AI in some corporate workflows. Investors got a number and a deadline, but production crews are still waiting to see how much work the combined company will actually have for them.

An August analysis commissioned by L.A. County estimated that the merger could put about 4,500 local film and TV jobs at risk over the next three years. That was an estimate of potential losses, not a count of people already laid off. It also preceded the fall settlement, which required additional production spending.

California and other states did get some promises in writing from Skydance. A court-approved settlement disclosed in an SEC filing requires the new company to release a minimum number of films in theaters and bars it from selling or closing the Paramount and Warner Bros. studio lots for five years. The deal also requires the company to spend at least $1.5 billion more on U.S. filmmaking over that period than the two companies would have spent at their combined pace in 2025.

But where will that business go? Curley, for one, can imagine some good coming out of an industry that’s scattered beyond Los Angeles. Maybe more people will get a shot at making something of their own. Maybe there’ll be a burst of indie filmmaking like the one he associates with the ’70s.

The Academy Award-winning craftsman can also see doors closing for people who’ve spent their working lives learning how to make movies.

“Maybe this is a good thing for the young people,” he said. “I’m hoping that’s the case anyway.”

It’s an oddly fitting coda for the “Whiplash” production designer, but still not the one he wants. For now, Curley already has an Oscar. What he needs is a call time and the chance to create again.

Two Major Developers Explain Why They Like And Dislike Making Games For Xbox Series S

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"I wish hardware like the 'Xbox Series S' wouldn't be released (laughs)"

Over the years, we've heard all kinds of stories from all kinds of developers about whether they either like or dislike making games for the Xbox Series S, and former Tekken boss Katsuhiro Harada falls into the "dislike" category.

In a recent panel discussion that was transcribed by the folks at InvenGlobal, Harada noticed that a senior Xbox official was in the audience, and joked that he wanted to let them know how much he isn't a fan of the Xbox Series S.

"Since a senior Xbox official is actually in attendance here today, I wish hardware like the 'Xbox Series S' wouldn't be released (laughs)."

He then went on to explain that although the low memory is "an issue" of the Xbox Series S, the hardest part is that it's actually so similar to the Series X, but with "awkwardly lower performance". Harada also pointed out that players direct their complaints to the developers rather than Microsoft if they don't like the visuals and/or performance.

"Low memory is an issue, but the hardest part is that it's a device so similar to Series X while having awkwardly lower performance [...] Furthermore, in markets like the US, there are more Series S users than Series X users, so we can't just cut corners. Yet after launch, Series S users direct their complaints at us developers rather than Microsoft, asking "Why is texture resolution lower only on Series S?"

Finally, he also noted that developing for the Nintendo Switch 2 is actually easier in some ways because it's a "distinct device for Nintendo fans", whereas the Xbox Series S is akin to "having to do extra work for a slightly weaker child" compared to putting in your "absolute best output" for the Xbox Series X.

Despite what it might seem like, a lot of this conversation seemed to be very lighthearted and full of laughs, and when Cyberpunk 2077's Paweł Sasko stepped in to highlight the benefits of the Xbox Series S including its affordable price and the ability to better optimise for low-spec platforms, Harada admitted that he made it sound a lot more attractive.

In fact, it was so convincing that Harada jokingly worried that it might lead to a similar Project Helix alternative!

Director Paweł: "Harada speaks a lot of truth. Players don't fully understand the technical context behind why fewer characters appear on screen or why visuals are downgraded. However, intensely optimizing for lower-spec platforms definitely brings the benefit of making the game run significantly better across all platforms—including PS5, high-end PCs, and handhelds like Steam Deck and ROG Ally.

And the biggest advantage of Series S is that its affordable price makes it accessible. Not every gamer can buy expensive high-end hardware, so from a developer's perspective, even if the strict constraints are painful, there is a silver lining in expanding market penetration to deliver the game to more players."

CEO Harada: "Director Paweł packaged Series S so gracefully that the Microsoft representative in the audience keeps nodding along. I'm worried they'll feel reassured and release a successor like 'Helix S' for the next-gen Xbox as well (laughs)."

It's best not to take any of this too seriously, but it does at least highlight the differing opinions throughout the industry about whether the Xbox Series S was a good or bad idea in hindsight.

From a consumer perspective at least, we reckon it was a fantastic idea. The Xbox Series S has continually managed to deliver incredible-looking games over the past six years, and in a time where console prices are constantly on the up, the Series S at least provides a somewhat affordable current-gen hardware option - at least if you find a good deal.

(those Devs would be wrong by the way, you meet your audience wherever they are and make great gameplay, not excuses)

Kvernadze costa solo 4 milioni di euro? La clausola c'è, ma è valida a partire da luglio

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Tra i giocatori che si stanno mettendo maggiormente in mostra nel Frosinone che è tra le rivelazioni della Serie A, occupa di diritto un posto l'esterno Giorgi Kvernadze (23 anni), autore fino a questo momento di 5 gol e 6 assist nelle 12 presenze che ha messo insieme con i ciociari tra Serie A e Coppa Italia.

Inevitabile che su un profilo come quello di Kvernadze, classe 2003 e di talento, si stiano posando attenzioni in vista del prossimo calciomercato, quello di gennaio. Si è parlato del Napoli, per quello che però sembra al momento giusto un lieve interesse, ma soprattutto della Roma, che sulla ricerca dell'esterno d'attacco fonderà la prossima sessione di trasferimenti. La valutazione del Frosinone - che intanto lavora al rinnovo - è elevata, supera i 20-25 milioni di euro, anche se esiste pure lo spauracchio di una certa clausola.

Cominciamo con un chiarimento: effettivamente nel contratto di Kvernadze con il Frosinone, in scadenza al 30 giugno 2028, è presente una clausola che permette di risolvere il vincolo con i gialloblù di fronte al pagamento di circa 4 milioni di euro di indennizzo. Una cifra al momento fortemente sproporzionata rispetto al reale valore del georgiano sul mercato, e anche per questo a Frosinone sembrano avere particolare premura di arrivare alla fumata bianca per il rinnovo (offerto 1 milione di euro netto l'anno, rispetto ai 200mila che percepisce ora). C'è però una specifica fondamentale, da fare, ovverosia che i gialloblù hanno ancora qualche mese a loro disposizione per arrivare a dama, considerando che la validità della suddetta clausola inizia solamente a partire da luglio.

Ford Recalling 33,000 Explorer Police Interceptors Because of Interior Lights That Might Not Turn Off

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our car experts choose every product we feature. We may earn money from the links on this page.

A software glitch can trigger a phantom “Global On” command, leaving the map lights illuminated even when officers try to turn them off.

Lights on a police car are extremely important. From flashing red and blue emergency lights to spotlights designed to illuminate whatever officers are investigating, they serve a variety of purposes. But sometimes, it's best if there are no lights on at all. That's why Ford Explorer Police Interceptors are equipped with a Police Dark Car feature: the last thing an officer wants during a nighttime stakeout is unwanted interior lighting. Unfortunately, more than 33,000 Explorer Police Interceptors have a problem that can leave their map lights stuck on, and it's led to a safety recall.

According to a National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) recall report, Ford is recalling 33,578 Explorer Police Interceptor vehicles from the 2026 and 2027 model years. The problem involves the overhead console, which can incorrectly interpret an electrical signal during vehicle startup as a command to turn on the map lights. Ford calls this a “phantom ‘Global On’ condition.”

Should this condition occur, not only will the map lights remain illuminated, but they can't be turned off using the individual switches. That can be annoying and distracting while driving, but it can be even more troublesome if the officers inside are trying to keep a low profile. The condition also violates Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standard (FMVSS) No. 101, Section S5.3.4(a). Under that standard, front-row interior lighting must have a means of being switched off.

Vehicles built between March 3, 2025, and October 23, 2026, are included in the recall. As of October 13, Ford received 69 warranty claims related to the defect. No crashes or injuries have been reported. Ford dealers will replace the overhead consoles in vehicles built before July 9, 2026, while newer vehicles will have their consoles inspected for an updated component. If the correct component is already installed, the console will simply be reinstalled; otherwise, it will be replaced. All repairs will be performed free of charge, as is required by federal law.

Owners are expected to receive notification letters between December 28 and December 30, 2026. In the meantime, police officers planning any nighttime stakeouts might want to use another vehicle until their Explorer has been repaired.

USA, Pochettino verso lo United? Robinson: "Il ct è concentrato sulle prossime 2 gare"

Le voci del possibile ritorno in Inghilterra, per sedere sulla panchina del Manchester United, di Mauricio Pochettino hanno ovviamente tenuto banco in casa Stati Uniti e nel ritiro della Nazionale impegnata a prepara la doppia sfida contro Haiti nei quarti di finale di Concacaf Nations League.

Il difensore statunitense Antonee Robinson ha parlato di questa situazione sottolineando come il ct sia concentrato sulle due gare di questa pausa e non sia cambiato nulla dopo la notizia di un suo sì ai Red Devils che potrebbe voler dire divorzio dalla nazionale nordamericana: “La notizia ci ha colti di sorpresa, come accaduto a livello globale. Cose simili sono già successe in passato, come durante i Mondiali, quando diversi club importantissimi hanno iniziato a valutare i possibili candidati per la nuova stagione. - prosegue il terzino in forza al Fulham – Avere un allenatore che ha questi contatti significa essere molto fortunati e lavorare con un tecnico di prim’ordine, ma non sta cambiando il lavoro che stiamo svolgendo.
