Chris Harrison Says Being Fired From ‘The Bachelor’ Was a “Blessing,” Not a Cancellation

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

And it "probably" had more to do with his contract negotiations than the internet thinks, Harrison tells The Hollywood Reporter ahead of the premiere of his new Fox Nation reality dating competition.

Chris Harrison is back as host of a TV dating competition, but he won’t be handing out any roses.

Harrison, the former longtime host of The Bachelor (and its in-universe spinoffs), is launching his own spin on the genre with Chris Harrison’s The Vow from Lionsgate Alternative Television, which streams on Fox News Channel’s companion platform Fox Nation. You’ll remember Harrison from getting fired from The Bachelor for defending 2021 contestant Rachael Kirkconnell for attending an antebellum-themed fraternity formal three years before she appeared on the ABC series. Related Stories TV 'The Bachelor' Returns, 'The Rookie' Moves as ABC Sets Midseason Schedule TV Laura Owens Sentenced to 30 Days in Jail for Pregnancy Fraud Targeting 'The Bachelor' Star Clayton Echard

Five long years later, Harrison is bringing his own (second) bride with him. Lauren Zima, the former Entertainment Tonight host, and Harrison were married in 2023. Harrison and Zima created The Vow together and executive produce it.

“It feels really a little cathartic,” Harrison told The Hollywood Reporter of his Hollywood comeback vehicle, which has more than flicked at his old job in its marketing materials — earning Lionsgate and Fox a cease and desist from The Bachelor studio Warner Bros. Television.

Chris Harrison’s The Vow curates the dates between its hero and his prospects, setting up “real-world compatibility challenges,” according to its synopsis. It’s all pretty faith-based and longterm focused: “At a time when much of modern dating culture is driven by fleeting connections and social media attention, the series offers a different approach by placing the focus on meaningful relationships, commitment and building a life together.”

The six-episode Chris Harrison’s The Vow premieres its first two episodes today on Fox Nation; read more from our interview with Harrison below.

Five years later, how do you now feel about your very public split with The Bachelor?

I know this sounds weird because it was so tumultuous and it was a crazy time in my life, but I look back at it as a bit of a blessing. I was in a contract negotiation with them. I was talking to my wife: “Is it time to move on? What do I do?” And I don’t know if this lightning-bolt moment hadn’t happened, if I would have stepped away. Having that divorce and that breakup was a blessing. It allowed me to step away. However humbling all of that was, it allowed me time to hit the pause button and watch my kids graduate high school, fall in love, get married in my own life — tell my own love story for a minute — and then kind of get the fire burning again and get excited about creating my own show because The Bachelor wasn’t my baby.

Being able to create this with my wife and with the people I choose to do it with, and be there every step of the way to executive produce, host, sit there and go through the post-production process — it is a painstaking process to get something there, and it’s a great achievement. We’re very proud of it and very excited.

Do you believe your contract situation played more of a role in you being let go from The Bachelor than was initially reported?

Probably. But in any divorce, you only know your side, right? The rest is speculation. They have their story, and then somewhere in the middle is probably the truth. I’m guessing it had some impact on it all, but how much I have no idea. It definitely played in it for me, and gave me that chance to break away from that, and come back and be so refreshed and excited and involved from the ground up and create [a new show]. Again, it’s weird to call something like that a blessing, but when you’re humbled and you move forward and you come back bigger and better, feel the excitement in the air — timing is everything, and the timing now is so good.

I don’t. I know people say that because of the time, and that term really came on strong then. I don’t say that, and I don’t think that. In my opinion, it makes me seem like a victim, and I wasn’t. I had a messy public divorce with the people I worked for. It was a very humbling moment for me, and I think a little humility and getting knocked down is good in life. Look, as I tell my kids, life’s not a hockey stick — it’s a roller coaster ride. The ups and the downs. I’m glad I don’t define my life by being the host of a certain show. Again, I go back to my faith. I go back to my friends and family, and you carry on and come back bigger and stronger and hopefully smarter. If you don’t learn from something like that, God, you’ve done yourself a disservice. I do feel like a better host, a better producer, a better husband, a better friend because of all that.

Do you feel that the industry has typecast you into this host-of-reality-dating-competition role? Or is this genuinely what you want to be doing right now?

It is. I got to a very blessed place in my life, and it’s very rare you can get there in business. And that is being able to choose the project, and also choose who you would like to do that project with. Going back to The Bachelor, I was a hired host, so I didn’t get to choose who I was working for, with, etc. You go in, you do your job, and you are so happy to get the paycheck in this business. But along the way, I did Who Wants to Be a Millionaire? I hosted Miss America, The Walking Wallendas.

I did a bunch of other shows, and really, what I love is twofold: I love hosting and producing TV. I find it so invigorating, intriguing. I love telling stories, being a part of stories — but I also love love. I’ll admit, I’m a little bit of a hopeless romantic. I love rom-coms — those are my favorite movies — and I love diving into these people’s lives, kind of exposing them and telling their stories and hopefully helping them find love, as romantic and silly as that may sound. I love being a part of those stories. When you see it work, that’s the addiction. When you see it work, you’re just like, man, that lightning in a bottle, that love story that we’re all clamoring for, that we all enjoy. It’s worth it. It makes it all worth it.

Forgive me for not remembering his name, but how did you find this show’s “bachelor”? He’s frustratingly handsome and seems like a good dude too.

Right and he has a cool fucking name. It’s not fair.

He could be a spy. He could be a stunt man. He might be the next James Bond. We we like to say he’s if Tim Tebow and [Rob Gronkowski] had a baby, that’s what Jace looks like. He has the looks, and yes, he has the abs. He really was that diamond in the rough, and that’s what set the tone.

We had this amazing casting director, Lindsay Liles, who was a producer on The Bachelor and left to go do her own casting agency. I had this great relationship with her, but she thinks like a producer, and that was really important to understand— as the kids say, she understood the assignment. She really knocked it out of the park, and we took more time, more energy, probably more money on casting than anything. We really wanted to do our due diligence on every aspect of these people, and make sure that this is who we wanted to launch this show with. In our case that diamond in the rough was from Canby, Oregon, of all places, and he is a man of faith, a man of values, high morals. Not perfect — he’s a human being. And he’s a little bit older. He’s 30 years old. That was important to us as well. The frontal lobe is formed, and he knows what he wants.

Faith is a big part of the show. Jace and his bachelorettes, for lack of a better word, pray together on camera. Fox Nation’s audience should be into that. Tell me about your own faith.

My wife and I — faith very much plays a part in our lives. It’s a big part of our marriage. I think it’s the foundation. Definitely as a man, it’s my foundation. It’s something I always fall back on. But our faith wasn’t the impetus for the show, but what I do think is, it made Lauren and I good stewards of how to create this environment.

That was why we ended up partnering with [Fox Nation] because that is part of their brand. They have other faith-based shows. All of us thought it was important that this wasn’t a cocktail joke. It’s not. “Hey, look, we’re doing a show about faith and look how crazy and silly this can be!” No, I wanted the intention there, and it was important that we understand that world to create the environment so they can actually feel able to let go. Like they— you know, I guess pun intended, they had to put their faith in us to come on this show, talk about their faith, feel comfortable doing that. And I think we were trusted to create that world for them that they could let go and really try to find love and talk about it freely.

So the season is wrapped as we speak. Did any of the 11 participants sort of expose themselves as maybe not being there for the right reasons?

What I was hoping for as a producer was, if we did a show involved that involved people of faith, that if we brought 11 humans on, we’d have 11 different opinions, experiences, and I was hoping that we would get the conversations that we have. And this is one thing I’m really excited about. You are going to see and hear conversations and topics covered you’ve never heard on reality TV, and it is raw. It’s rare. You’re going to see people pray, and you will see people called into question about their faith.

Some people, like the people at Warner Bros., will call this a ripoff of The Bachelor, and I believe it’s a fair criticism. That said, pretty much every dating show since The Bachelor has been, to some degree, a ripoff of The Bachelor. You, more than most people, should get a pass on that. But you’ve removed a bunch of the tropes, like a formal elimination ceremony to end each episode, and Jace is not expected to propose in the finale…

We took all those hurdles and safeguards that honestly, you probably should have as a producer, because it’s smart. Like an elimination ceremony. “OK, it’s 10 o’clock. Kick two girls out, so I know I can end episode one with a break up.” We didn’t do that. We felt any contrived, produced moment is going to [feel like a contrived, produced moment].

Well episode one does kind of end with a break up, but I’ll concede that the elimination feels more authentic. Jace’s marching orders are to eliminate a potential mate when she stops seeming like a potential mate.

It’s what a gentleman should do. It’s what women should expect. If you’re going to break up, look her in the eye. Don’t shoot a text. Don’t go on your keyboard. Don’t just ghost somebody. Let’s raise the level, guys — come on!

But that intentionality is very important to us, but it’s scary because if you take your hand off the wheel, you don’t know what you’re going to get. But what we found was the unpredictability is the drama. You don’t know when those breakups are going to happen, and even as producers, Jace would be talking, and all of a sudden we’d be like, “Whoa, OK, he’s up right now. Here we go.” And it turned out to be great TV.

In the sizzle reel for the remainder of the season, it is revealed to (those of us who don’t watch The Bachelor) that one of the women was on The Bachelor. I’m guessing that was after your time, so did you know?

Yeah, yeah. Yeah. We talked about that in casting, and we had a long talk between all the executive producers and our casting agent. And again, it was very intentional. Why her faith in where she is in her journey and what she wants, and it felt like none of that was really given a fair shake [on The Bachelor], and she wasn’t really able to talk about that and show that. So we thought, “You know what? OK.” And we had a long talk with her, and we felt like that was a very intentional, good choice to add to the mix.

Was it a challenging decision to cast the woman who sells penile implants for a faith-based Fox Nation show?

It’s what she does for a living. I wanted to show what people of faith really look like in this world. This generation, these are women are unbelievably dynamic, incredibly diverse, and come from all walks of life. One is a penile implant salesman. So be it. That’s her job. She’s really — and I mean this sincerely — she’s passionate about it.

No spoilers, but are you happy with the arc of the season and the results, whatever they may be?

I am. I am very proud of the show. We are very proud of the show. We’re proud of what we created. On one side, because this business is so hard, and to get a show created, sold, and produced, and on air…it’s tough right now. And to put people to work and all those things. There’s that side of it that we’re really proud of. But then the product we created, the show, the idea we had — to watch that come to fruition really was a dream. The show’s not predicated on the fact that it’s going to work. It’s not guaranteed.

Chris Harrison’s The Vow streams weekly on Wednesdays on Fox Nation.

ZWO introduces the Seestar S30 Plus

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

ZWO has added a new entry point to its smart telescope lineup. The Seestar S30 Plus went on sale Nov. 10 for $499 — $474 for a limited time — undercutting the S30 Pro by $200 and making it the cheapest Seestar available.

“Many people are interested in astrophotography but hesitate when they see the equipment and setup traditionally involved,” ZWO founder Sam Wen said in a press release. “With S30 Plus, we wanted to give beginners more room to explore and create without taking away the simplicity that makes Seestar easy to start with.”

Since the original S50 debuted in April 2023, the Seestar line has continued to grow, most recently with this year’s 4K S30 Pro and S50 Pro. The S30 Plus heads in the opposite direction from the Pro models — and with the original S30 reportedly discontinued, it now holds down the entry-level slot.

Effectively, the new Plus model is an S30 Pro with a few features stripped out to meet a lower price. Like the Pro, it carries two cameras: a telephoto for galaxies, nebulae, and other deep-sky targets; and a wide-angle for broad sweeps of the night sky. The telephoto camera keeps the Pro’s 1.2-inch (30 millimeters) aperture and four-element apochromatic lens, including an element of extra-low-dispersion (ED) glass.

The biggest change is to the sensors. Both of the Plus’ cameras shoot 1440×2560 images (3.7 megapixels, or roughly 2K), versus the S30 Pro’s 8.3-megapixel 4K output — still a step up from the original S30’s 2.1 megapixels. But the difference in sensor size doesn’t mean less detail — only a difference in field of view. The Plus has the same pixel size as the Pro, so zooming in on your photos reveals the same level of detail.d

RELATED: Review: ZWO Seestar S50 smart telescope

On the telephoto side, the Plus swaps the Pro’s larger Sony sensor for a 1/1.8-inch SmartSens SC485SL chip. Both have the same 2.9-micrometer pixels behind the same 160 mm lens, so the Pro’s bigger chip just spans more sky: about 4.6°, compared with the Plus’ 3°. That’s still enough to comfortably frame targets like the Pleiades star cluster and the Orion and Rosette nebulae, along with the Moon’s full disk — or the Sun’s, with the included solar filter in place.

The wide-angle camera uses the same 1/2-inch Sony IMX586 sensor as the Pro, behind a lens with identical specs (6 mm, f/1.75). But where the Pro takes in a 63° view, the Plus crops it down to the same 2K format as its telephoto sensor, covering a smaller 43.5° field — still wide enough for the Milky Way, whole constellations, star trails, and time-lapses.

The Plus does give up a few of the S30 Pro’s handier features. The Pro can run both cameras at once, stacking a nebula through the telephoto while the wide-angle captures the Milky Way and landscape around it; the Plus only shoots with one at a time. Its wide-angle camera also can’t stitch Milky Way panoramas, though a telephoto mosaic mode for larger deep-sky targets remains. And onboard storage is halved to 64 GB.

In the box, buyers get the scope, a tabletop tripod, a carrying case, a USB-C cable, and a magnetic solar filter. The 3.6-pound (1.65 kilograms) unit runs up to 6 hours on a charge and has UV/IR-cut and duo-band light-pollution filters built in.

Like every Seestar, the S30 Plus is driven entirely from a phone through the Seestar app. Users simply pair the device to their phone, let it calibrate, and then pick a target — or let the app suggest one. The S30 Plus automatically slews to the target, centers it, and begins tracking and live stacking, with the image building in detail on screen as frames accumulate.

The app can also identify and label constellations in the wide-angle view, and it can queue up automated imaging plans to run through the night. Finished shots can be tweaked and shared straight from the phone. For those who’d rather process elsewhere, the scope saves stills as JPEG and FITS files and video as MP4 and AVI.

The Seestar S30 Plus is available now for $499 through Seestar’s and ZWO’s online stores, Amazon in the U.S. and Europe, and authorized dealers worldwide. It’s now the cheapest way into the Seestar ecosystem — and owners who outgrow it can easily step up to a Pro model.

More details are available at ZWO’s product page.

Flo Health Earns ISO 13485 Certification for Its Quality Management System

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

ISO 13485 certification validates the quality management system of the women's health app against the global standard for medical devices

LONDON, Nov. 11, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Flo Health, the #1 women's health app used by more than 500 million people worldwide, has achieved ISO 13485 certification, following an independent audit of its quality management system conducted by Scarlet, a government-accredited certification body specialized in AI medical devices. This means an independent third-party auditor has verified the integrity of our quality management system (QMS) behind how we would design, build and maintain medical device software.

ISO 13485 is the internationally recognized standard for QMS in the medical device industry. It requires organizations to demonstrate consistent control over design, risk management, supplier oversight, traceability, and preventative and corrective action.

Flo's QMS runs on Ketryx, an intelligent lifecycle platform for regulated product development, which provides continuous compliance and end-to-end traceability from requirements through risk, verification and release.

As healthcare organizations and patients increasingly rely on digital health tools and connected devices, independent verification of quality practices is essential to trust and provides users with assurance that products are developed and maintained to the highest standards.

Quality, safety, and privacy are cornerstone to how Flo operates. Flo also holds ISO 27001 and ISO 27701 certifications for its information security and privacy management systems. Flo's latest ISO 13485 certification came back with no major findings.

Achieving ISO 13485 marks the next step in Flo's development, as independently verifying each innovation is designed with quality and safety in mind. Unlike a one-time assessment, maintaining ISO 13485 requires ongoing independent oversight, including annual surveillance audits and full recertification every three years.

"Rigorous quality for the benefit of our users is paramount to how Flo operates," said Tauseef Mehrali, VP of Regulatory Affairs for Flo. "ISO 13485 certification is independent validation that our processes are built to meet the standards regulators and customers hold us to. It gives us a stronger foundation to keep improving the value and caliber of our infrastructure and policies."

Certification is only one part of how Flo earns and keeps customer trust. We build quality and risk management principles directly into product development, where privacy and security are seen as foundational business drivers.

A certified QMS is the foundation for developing software as a medical device. It demonstrates disciplined design controls and risk management, underpins the conformity assessment route for CE and UKCA marking, and closely aligns with the U.S. Food and Drug Administration's Quality Management System Regulation, which incorporates ISO 13485 by reference. It gives Flo the foundation to extend the same rigor to the regulated digital health products in the future.

About Flo HealthFlo Health is the #1 Health and Fitness app worldwide and the #1 OB-GYN-recommended platform for period and cycle tracking. In 2024, Flo became the first European femtech unicorn following an investment from General Atlantic and now supports 80 million monthly active users (MAUs) worldwide. Powered by a network of over 100+ medical experts, Flo guides women through every stage of their health journey â from menstruation to conception, pregnancy, and menopause â offering curated cycle and ovulation tracking, personalized health insights, daily educational content, and a private community for users to share questions or concerns. Through its Pass it on Project, Flo has donated more than 29M Flo Premium subscriptions to improve health literacy to women in need, aiming to reach up to one billion. A leader in privacy and security, Flo Health's Anonymous Mode feature was recognized as one of TIME's Best Inventions 2023 and also named a finalist for Fast Company's 2023 World Changing Ideas Awards as part of the company's commitment to privacy.

For more information, please visit https://flo.health.

XRP Price Prediction: Brad Garlinghouse Faces $1.05 Test as Whales Buy $72M

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

XRP whales accumulated $72 million over four days, but its price remains dangerously close to critical support. Meanwhile, spot XRP ETFs recorded $10.4 million in net outflows, their largest single-day withdrawal since March.

XRP is hovering around $1.05, a level that could determine its next major move. With selling pressure mounting, Ripple CEO Brad Garlinghouse faces another test of confidence in the token.

Garlinghouse has spent years positioning Ripple as a major player in institutional payments. Its ambitions extend across cross-border transfers, financial infrastructure, and institutional adoption.

However, XRP holders still need those developments to translate into stronger token demand. Ripple’s business expansion and XRP’s market performance are two different things. That gap has widened as investors reconsider their exposure to established crypto. Institutional partnerships can strengthen Ripple’s business without automatically triggering additional XRP purchases.

The latest whale accumulation offers some encouragement. Large holders adding $72 million worth of XRP suggests certain investors see value near current levels. But ETF withdrawals tell a different story. The $10.4 million outflow shows institutional demand remains inconsistent, even as whales accumulate.

Bitcoin’s investment narrative is gradually expanding beyond digital gold. Developers are increasingly exploring ways to bring faster transactions, decentralized finance, and additional applications into its ecosystem.

That shift could create opportunities for infrastructure projects targeting Bitcoin’s limitations. Bitcoin Layer 2 networks aim to make the underlying ecosystem more useful without replacing Bitcoin itself.

Bitcoin Hyper is one project pursuing this direction. Its approach combines Bitcoin-focused infrastructure with Solana Virtual Machine execution, targeting faster transactions and broader application development.

The project has raised $33.2 million at its current presale stage. Its current token price stands at a low $0.0136, with staking rewards reaching 35% APY. But its presale is scheduled to conclude on November 26, putting a deadline on the current entry opportunity.

XRP represents an established payments-focused crypto, while Bitcoin Hyper targets an emerging infrastructure opportunity.

For investors seeking exposure to earlier-stage projects, the potential appeal lies in entering before an ecosystem reaches maturity. Established cryptocurrencies offer greater market recognition, but emerging infrastructure projects can attract attention through their expansion potential.

Bitcoin Hyper’s pitch centers on making Bitcoin more functional. That proposition could appeal to investors who believe Bitcoin’s next growth chapter will involve more than simply holding BTC.

The immediate XRP price outlook remains heavily dependent on the $1.05 support level. Losing this floor could expose XRP to another wave of selling. The next downside target sits around $0.99. A move below psychological support could further weaken confidence among short-term traders.

However, XRP’s Relative Strength Index has fallen to approximately 24. That places the indicator deep inside oversold territory, suggesting selling pressure has become unusually intense.

Oversold conditions can create opportunities for a relief rally, particularly when large holders continue accumulating. But buyers still need to demonstrate enough strength to reclaim lost resistance levels.

A recovery toward $1.30 would provide an initial bullish target. Stronger momentum could subsequently bring the $1.50 area back into focus.

For now, the XRP price faces two competing forces. Whale accumulation and oversold conditions offer reasons for optimism, while ETF outflows and fragile support keep downside risks alive. Defending $1.05 could give bulls another opportunity to rebuild momentum. Losing it would put $0.99 firmly on the radar.

What to Expect From Best Buy's 2026 Black Friday and Cyber Monday Sales

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

For all intents and purposes, Black Friday sales are already here. There are plenty of deals out already, even though we're weeks away from the actual event. One of the reasons for that is the competition retailers have with each other to be the first to get your money. One of the retailers that is making the biggest early splash is Best Buy, confirming their entire plan for the rest of the year. Here's what you need to know.

Best Buy has announced the following events:

DoorBoosters: A Halloween sale from Oct. 30 to Nov. 1.

Early Black Friday Deals and Doorbusters: The official start of early Black Friday deals on Nov. 6. New deals will drop every Friday.

Black Friday Doorbusters and Deals: Best Buy says this will be its main sale, starting on Thursday, Nov. 19 and running until Nov. 28 with deals on "computing, gaming, home theater, wearables, and more."

Cyber Sunday and Monday: Best Buy is extending Cyber Monday to five days and says you can find deals going up to 50% off select tech.

Last-minute savings: The Christmas sale will run from Dec. 18 until Dec. 24.

You don't need to be any kind of member to shop Best Buy's Black Friday in July sale; however, it is free to sign up for a My Best Buy membership, which will get you free shipping. If you join one of their paid subscriptions (My Best Buy Plus for $49.99 per year or My Best Buy Total for $179.99 per year), you'll get faster free shipping, exclusive prices and deals, an extended 60-day return window on most products, and access to select sales, like the Member Deals Days sale that is going on right now. Paid members will also earn extra rewards from both the current sale and the upcoming Black Friday in July sale.

You can find deals on TVs, laptops, headphones, video games, electric bikes, and more. There will be new deals every week in stores, on its website, and in the Best Buy mobile app. They'll offer price matching, meaning if the price drops further during the sale (Dec. 24), they will match it and offer a refund.

Here are the best deals I've found so far. I'll be updating these deals throughout the sale, so make sure to come back and see what's new. Remember that new deals drop every day.

Best Buy price matches its own products, so if a product drops in price later, you'd get those savings too, but it does not cover any deals coming from a competitor's Black Friday sale. There are some other important exceptions you can read in more detail here.

In short, yes, Black Friday still offers discounts that can be rare throughout the rest of the year. If there’s something you want to buy, or you’re shopping for gifts, it’s a good time to look for discounts on what you need, especially tech sales, home improvement supplies, and fitness tech. Of course, if you need to save money, the best way to save is not to buy anything.

The other major retailers doing Black Friday sales are Amazon, Target, and Walmart. Currently, Amazon has many early deals, seeing their lowest prices yet. Target has some week-long deals and a Deal of the Day. Walmart will have three Black Friday and Cyber Monday shopping events. Nowadays, large retailers and small businesses compete for Cyber Monday shoppers, so you can expect practically every store to run sales through Monday, Nov. 30, 2026.

No. If you find the product you're looking for with a major discount right now, get it. It's not likely to drop any lower, but it could always run out of stock. Take advantage of price matching or guarantees instead of waiting. Remember, you can always return products later if you change your mind. For the majority of products, the deal from Black Friday will be the same one on Cyber Monday.

I Think DC’s TV Series Have Struggled With A Lot Of The Same Problems Netflix Shows Have

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

An Internet User Just Made A Comment About James Gunn’s DC Tenure That Makes Me Feel Sad And Weird

Fan Reactions On James Gunn’s Goodbye Post Made Lanterns And DC Points I’m Also Concerned About

James Gunn Would Like You To Know He’s Not Trying To Recreate The MCU (Even With Superman)

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It's been a wild week for fans of the superhero genre, especially folks who have been watching the DC movies in order. The internet exploded when it was revealed that James Gunn was stepping away at co-CEO of DC Studios, with the public left wondering if we'll ever see his first phase of announced projects called Gods and Monsters. There's been a ton of discourse online, although I think the issue with the DC TV shows (which are streaming with a HBO Max subscription) have the same issue as some Netflix series.

Gunn's DCU might be cooked, and there are countless questions about upcoming DC movies and TV shows. Variety wrote a long story about what's reportedly happening behind the scenes, as well as the perceived missteps of the shared universe. Regarding what happened on the small screen, the publication blamed the three-year wait between seasons of Peacemaker for the sophomore entry being less popular than the first.

I personally loved how Peacemaker Season 2 retconned the Justice League and helped establish the new DCU, but not everyone was quite as invested as me. Fans have bee wondering if Peacemaker might get a Season 3, although that seems more unlikely with Gunn out.

While all eyes are on what DC did or didn't do wrong, I think that this symptom is something that's also happened for plenty of shows that are available with a Netflix subscription. We've seen a number of years pass between season of Stranger Things, Wednesday, Squid Games and more. While those are some of the streaming service's most popular projects, there are fans who dropped off or lost interest due to the long gap between seasons. Considering how many TV shows many of us watch, those years result in fans not really remembering what had happened during previous seasons.

Netflix also has a penchant for cancelling shows after just one season, and that very well might be happening at DC as well. Fans have been wondering if we'll get Lanterns Season 2, and concerns over the show's future have only gotten louder now that James Gunn and Peter Safran are out. Creature Commandos also hit HBO back in 2024, and there's been no indication that the animated series will get another batch of episodes. While folks are dissecting ever decision the Guardians of the Galaxy filmmaker made as co-CEO of DC Studios, I can't help but notice connective tissue with some problems that have followed Netflix.

Only time will tell what Gunn's replacement Former Marvel Studios executive and producer Jeremy Latcham is cooking up at DC. Fans are nervous about what's coming next, especially since we're still so early into the run of the DCU.

The next DC movie hitting theaters on Man of Tomorrow, directed by Gunn and releasing on July 9, 2027. What comes next for the shared universe after that sequel's release is anyone's guess.

Chelsea urged to sign Trent Alexander-Arnold amid Liverpool return rumours

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chelsea have been urged to sign ‘top-class’ Trent Alexander-Arnold in January following a frustrating start to his Real Madrid career.

Alexander-Arnold completed a dream move to Madrid last year, ending a 21-year spell at boyhood club Liverpool in which he won two Premier League titles and the Champions League.

The England international featured in a little over half of Real Madrid’s league games last season as the Spanish giants endured a rare trophyless campaign.

Competition at right-back only increased over the summer with the arrival of Denzel Dumfries from Inter Milan and Jose Mourinho is currently playing midfielder Federico Valverde in that position.

As a result Alexander-Arnold appears to be third choice and has only earned four starts this season, with his lack of game-time raising speculation over his future at the Bernabeu.

Read exclusive analysis of the biggest stories in football with Metro's weekly football newsletter In The Mixer. Sign up now for free.

A return to Liverpool has been raised as a potential possibility given his former club’s issues at right-back, but Glen Johnson has urged Chelsea to bring the 28-year-old to Stamford Bridge.

Chelsea’s first-choice right-back, club captain Reece James, is expected to be out of action until at least mid-January after undergoing knee surgery last month.

‘Trent Alexander-Arnold will definitely be frustrated playing such a small role at Real Madrid this season,’ ex-Liverpool and Chelsea defender Johnson told SeatPick.

‘Before he went to Madrid, he would have planned on nailing down his spot at right back there, and then they brought in a new right back the summer after he arrived, which will have only frustrated him further.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

‘Trent is a top-class player, and no top-class player wants to sit on the bench, he wants to be playing regular football.

‘I think he should be a target for Chelsea in January. I think they could get him on loan, and it would be perfect for both parties. Chelsea needs a right back at the moment, and Trent needs minutes under his belt.

‘We’ve seen how effective he can be in the Premier League, and if Xabi Alonso could get him back to his best, it would be a great deal to solve a problem for Chelsea and to get his career back on track.’

Liverpool legend Steven Gerrard recently said he would welcome Alexander-Arnold back to Anfield despite the fact his exit upset many fans.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

‘The decision he made and all the stuff around it, do I think it would be the right thing for Liverpool’s group now and the supporters? I’m going to sit on the fence and say I’m unsure,’ Gerrard told The Overlap Daily.

‘Ability-wise, talent-wise, what he’d done for Liverpool in terms of the success, I would say yes, but the ending and that still being a little bit raw…

‘I would, yes, myself personally. So I won’t sit on the fence because that’s poor from me. I would, yes.

‘Do I think it’s the right thing for this group now, this manager now and where Liverpool are at right now? I’m unsure. Does he make the Liverpool team and squad better? He does, 100 per cent.

‘It’s tough, it’s tough. I would, yes, and ability-wise, yes, I’m just not sure it’s right for the group, right now.’

Liverpool are fourth in the Premier League heading into the November international break, one place and two points above Chelsea.

Alexander-Arnold’s current club Real Madrid are second in La Liga, having recently closed the gap on leaders Barcelona to just three points with an El Clasico win at the Nou Camp.

Google’s Tabletop lets groups build AI planning tools together

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The Google Labs experiment turns a prompt into polls, itineraries and games a whole group can edit live.

Google Labs has launched Tabletop, an experiment that builds custom tools such as trip itineraries, dinner polls and expense splitters from a prompt. Groups share one space and edit it together in real time. It is open to US adults via a waitlist.

Google has launched Tabletop, an experiment from its Google Labs team that lets groups build their own planning tools with AI. Google announced the tool on Wednesday.

Users add links, files and ideas to a shared space, then ask @tabletop to build something. It can make trip itineraries, dinner polls, expense splitters and tournament brackets. There are also starter templates.

The creator shares an invite link in a group chat, and everyone can then use the space at the same time. Google compares it to working together in a Google Doc. Group members can vote, plan and track progress in one place.

The tools can also change as plans do. Members can ask @tabletop to change a rule or add something new without starting over. In one example on Google’s product page, a Pong game becomes Pickle Pong, with a bouncing pickle and a live scoreboard.

Google suggests uses such as road trips, sports leagues, weddings, weekly meal plans and school pickups. A chore tracker can even become a game with points and a shared reward.

Tabletop is rolling out in the US to adults aged 18 and over through a waitlist, with new users let in each week. Google has not said which AI model powers it.

Google Labs experiments sometimes become full products. NotebookLM started there and was renamed Gemini Notebook this year. Google has also opened its Play Store to AI-built apps, while Slack now lets teams build with AI agents inside its chat app.

J. Crew debuts new AI shopping tool that’s designed to think like a stylist

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Over the course of four decades, J. Crew’s style has woven cashmere cardigans, tweed jackets, and other preppy staples that have attracted high-profile fans including Michelle Obama, Demi Moore, and Meghan Markle.

Throughout the apparel brand’s history—beginning as a catalog, then growing to become a physical and omnichannel retailer—those styling decisions were made by humans. But beginning today, there’s artificial intelligence at work too.

On Wednesday, J. Crew announced a new consumer-facing, AI-enabled tool called Style Advisor, which runs on a collection of Anthropic’s models. Style Advisor is trained on data from thousands of outfit pairings from the company’s photo shoots and J. Crew’s styling principles, outfit and accessories pairings.

“It’s not just about the piece that you’re looking for, but how to style it,” says Danielle Schmelkin, chief information officer at J. Crew. “What those stylists bring to the table—and really recommend more than just the item that you may have picked up when you walked into the store—we are really looking to create that online.”

As more shoppers gravitate to agentic shopping, with more than 50% of global consumers using AI to buy goods within the past three months, new tools have proliferated ranging from Meta Platforms’ Muse agent and Alexa for Shopping from Amazon to AI shopping assistants created by retailers including Lowe’s, Ulta Beauty, and Macy’s.

But while 56% of consumers say they use AI chat or search tools including Anthropic’s Claude or OpenAI’s ChatGPT, only 27% are gravitating to retailer-created AI shopping assistants, according to consulting firm McKinsey.

“I think the visual piece is really important,” says Schmelkin of the distinctive edge that she promises Style Advisor will offer. In the future, the tool will also pull in data from a consumer’s past purchase history, allowing Style Advisor to make even more personalized recommendations, replicating that’s already possible during the omnichannel shopping experience.

She says Style Advisor is also grounded in contextual details, asking questions to better understand a shopper’s intent. If a J. Crew customer were to tell it that they were looking for an outfit to attend a wedding—in, say, Miami, but not too formal—the tool will make recommendations based on these nuances.

Style Advisor will debut on J. Crew’s desktop website and is expected to later be added to the retailer’s mobile app.

J. Crew’s Style Advisor runs on a variety of Claude’s models, including Fable 5.1, which is leveraged to handle the more complex and ambiguous judgment that allows the tool to think and respond like a stylist. Claude’s Sonnet 5.5 and Haiku 4.5 support the speed and efficiency of the back-and-forth responses between consumers and the AI stylist, while Claude Opus 5.5 was utilized by J. Crew’s team to build a working prototype.

“What is the actual experience that we want the shopper to have, and then, how do we think about making sure that we’re mapping the right models to be able to deliver that,” says Lauren Collett, Anthropic’s enterprise go-to-market leader.

Schmelkin says J. Crew built in a back-end observation layer that allows the retailer to closely monitor the costs. “We want to make sure we are constantly refining and using these models in the right way, both in terms of the outputs for our customers, but also the efficiency we want to see to make this scalable,” says Schmelkin.

Internally at J. Crew, to bolster AI adoption, the retailer has created an AI fluency program that begins with the basics and advances up through vibe coding. An internal AI lab, called “Second Thread,” began with just the tech team but has broadened to incorporate all corporate employees to discuss AI use cases and propose other technology solutions.

For four years, J. Crew has also run a two-day tech summit that features workshops, hackathons, guest speakers, and vendor presentations. Initially, this programming was only available to the tech team, but J. Crew has more recently democratized access. Employees are also encouraged to use AI enterprise tools from Anthropic and OpenAI.

The Style Advisor was conceptualized by a J. Crew digital product designer, who developed a working prototype before engineers were pulled into the project. The fact that the tool wasn’t mandated from the top of J. Crew’s C-suite reflects Schmelkin’s efforts to encourage employees to chart their own path on AI.

“I’ve been screaming to anyone who will listen for years now to get in there and build a habit,” says Schmelkin. “You have to find your a-ha moment and then all of a sudden…this could really change the way you think about your own life, work, and team productivity.”

Nvidia’s shares rattled by CEO's affair and board probe. The biggest tech headlines swirling this week centered on an admission over the weekend that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang had been unfaithful to his wife and that the AI chip maker’s board was investigating reports that he had been targeted by a business executive who U.S. intelligence says was a Chinese spy. Nvidia’s shares slipped on Monday as U.S. lawmakers are also asking for more information. Fortune reports that Nvidia controls about 90% of the market for high-powered GPU computer chips used in AI data centers. And yet another issue is poor succession planning by the board: Huang, who has been CEO for 33 years, has 60 direct reports and no clear successor.

Alphabet, meanwhile, takes aim at Nvidia. On Monday, Google debuted new AI chips that Bloomberg reports are intended to take some share from Nvidia’s dominant position and represent an acceleration in product development coming from the company’s chip division. But another corner of the search giant’s AI business is facing mounting criticism. Politicians are lamenting the company’s decision to block search for K-12 student accounts if districts have enacted AI bans. Sen. Edward Markey has demanded that the company reverse a policy; while Google says its AI features are so interconnected with search that it cannot offer the service without AI.

Anthropic continues to advance plans for IPO; debuts new frontier models. Yet another AI company aiming to navigate more turbulent times is Anthropic, which is reportedly planning to go public next week even as shares of AI companies have faced pressure as investors fret about high infrastructure costs and U.S. midterm election results that are divisively more “anti AI.” Separately, on Monday, Anthropic debuted two new AI models called Fable 5.2 and Mythos 5.2, which the company said generate outputs that are 30% faster than their predecessors. The models also cost 20% less than the prior generation. And on Tuesday, it unveiled a suite of new office productivity tools aimed at knowledge workers, putting Anthropic in closer competition with Microsoft.

OpenAI raises about $11 billion in a new funding round. The ChatGPT maker’s latest effort to raise more funds reportedly closed on Friday, with the remainder of the round, which could hit as much as $20 billion in total, expected to be completed “in the coming weeks,” according to media reports. OpenAI has reportedly sought out to raise $30 billion or more at a valuation of $1.4 trillion, but settled for a lower figure and a valuation of $1.25 trillion, amid the pressure AI companies are facing from investors. More optimistically, Business Insider reports on fresh research data from Graphite that found ChatGPT has captured 81% of worldwide AI chatbot traffic, easily outpacing rivals Google and Anthropic, who had been making some inroads.

Data and analytics, cyber jobs will see the largest headcount increase in 2027. AI’s impact on jobs have loomed over the IT industry, magnified by younger, entry-level workers that have taken to social media to lament they can’t find their first role and as Meta, Microsoft, Amazon, and other tech giants have announced layoffs. Yet encouragingly, 40% of enterprises plan to increase their IT headcount in 2027, according to a survey of 2,002 CIOs and other tech leaders conducted by Gartner. The biggest changes will be for data and analytics and cybersecurity roles.

Alicia Mullery, VP analyst at Gartner, tells Fortune that much of the concern about AI in the workplace is that these systems will save organizations so much time, they’ll need less people. But that’s not always the case: AI can create a lot of so-called “slop” that workers need to rummage through. And, even when the outputs are more advantageous, there are still plenty of instances where human-machine collaboration is needed.

IT departments tend to be further along on their AI journey and this is where CIOs can continue to step in and guide, remaking workflows across other parts of the business, says Mullery. “I see the CIO as really being at the forefront of being able to say, ‘Hey our teams went through this first,’” adds Mullery. “Here’s what we learned, here’s what worked, and here’s what didn’t work.”

- FFB Bank is seeking a CIO, a remote-based role. Posted salary range: $275K-$375K/year.

- Citi is seeking a head of retail and wealth risk technology, based in Rutherford, New Jersey. Posted salary range: $250K-$500K/year.

- DBV Technologies is seeking a VP of IT, based in Bernards, New Jersey. Posted salary range: $300K-$350K/year.

- The American Board of Emergency Medicine is seeking a CIO, based in East Lansing, Michigan. Posted salary range: $200K-$250K/year.

- Hyatt named Danny Miles to serve as the hospitality company’s chief digital and information officer, leading a newly combined organization that includes engineering, digital products, enterprise technology, data, AI, and cybersecurity. Miles most recently served as CTO at athletic gear maker Under Armour and also was CTO at Dollar Shave Club and a VP at Nike.

- Fox Factory promoted Troy Henige to the newly established role of CTO, leading the manufacturer’s enterprise technology and product roadmap strategies. Henige spent seven years in engineering roles at motorcycle and ATV suspension manufacturer Progressive Suspension before joining Fox a decade ago, most recently serving as VP of engineering.

- Time appointed Michael Dugan to the role of CTO. He joins the media company from peer G/O Media, where he served as CTO. Previously, Dugan served as CTO of Hearst Magazines and spent 17 years at Forbes, including four years as CTO.

- Authentic Brands Group promoted Adnan Somani to the role of CTO, expanding his responsibilities to include overseeing the brand management company’s AI strategy and IT organization. Somani joined Authentic Brands as EVP of digital technology in May 2024 and previously, founded the technology startup Aiva Labs.

- Presidio announced Doran Stienike will join the IT services firm as CIO, effective November 9. Previously, Stienike served as CIO and chief information security officer at software-as-a-service provider CSG International. He also served as CIO and held other technology leadership roles at ConAgra Foods.

- OneTrust named Tim Mullen to serve as CIO, overseeing the governance and compliance software firm’s enterprise technology strategy and accelerating its AI efforts. Mullen joined OneTrust in 2022 and previously served as CISO. He also held information security leadership roles at Change Healthcare, InterContinental Hotels Group, and Travelport.

- DNSFilter announced the appointment of Justin Foster as CTO. Foster joins the cybersecurity company from another cyber firm, Forescout, where he most recently served as CTO and oversaw the research and development organization. He joined Forescout through its acquisition of Cysiv, where he was co-founder and CTO.

Absolutely Everything You Need to Know About the Citizen Watch Catalog

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The Japanese brand offers endless variety, appealing affordability, and incredible technological innovation.

Whether you encounter them in multi-brand boutiques or stand-alone stores; in duty-free shops while on vacation; on the internet or at auction, Citizen’s watches are everywhere.

Founded in 1918, the brand that began with the simple objective of producing a Japanese-made watch has evolved into a truly global juggernaut, with annual turnover of approximately $2.1B for the fiscal year that ended in March of 2026. Vertically integrated, it produces everything from mechanical divers of the traditional ilk to tech-forward, satellite-guided modern tool watches that update time zones instantly as one zig-zags across the world. Its light-powered Eco-Drive technology essentially constitutes a movement category in its own right, while its wafer-thin Eco-Drive One models—some of the brand’s highest-end wares—command luxury prices.

Like Seiko—another homegrown Japanese brand—Citizen’s considerable scale and power has compounded over more than a century. And also like Seiko, it has grown its manufacturing capabilities to such an extent that it has successfully expanded into industries other than watchmaking. But where Citizen truly shines is in offering a remarkably capable and robust product at a price that elicits few complaints from the watch-buying public. Available in points of sale in roughly 130 countries—500 alone in the U.S.—Citizen’s timepieces are not only affordable, but they’re also truly ubiquitous.

For someone in search of a relatively inexpensive watch—any type of watch, really, save perhaps a high complication in precious metal with a dial made by a lone monk living in the Swiss Jura—that will hold up under adverse conditions, Citizen is a natural answer. Whether you’re looking for a feature-laden dive watch (Promaster Aqualand) or an elegant, ultra-thin dress watch (Eco-Drive One); a luxurious, design-forward everyday watch (Series 8) or a vintage-inspired take on a Genta-esque design (Zenshin), there’s a good chance that there’s a Citizen timepiece that will satisfy your desires.

The only issue is parsing the firm’s gigantic catalog in search of an ideal choice. To help you in this regard, we produced this handy guide.

While not as old as Seiko, Citizen has a long and storied watchmaking history filled with a staggering amount of technological innovation. In fact, it wouldn’t be too bold to claim that in a world based primarily around centuries-old technology, Citizen is one of the most forward-thinking marques.

The brand’s story begins in 1918, when entrepreneur Kamekichi Yamazaki established the Shokosha Watch Research Institute in Tokyo with the goal of providing a domestically produced alternative to imported Swiss and American watches. By 1924, it had produced its first pocket watch, which it named “Citizen” at the behest of Tokyo mayor Count Shinpei Goto, who imagined a watch available to ordinary members of society. Six years later, the Shokosha Watch Research Institute was officially incorporated as the Citizen Watch Company, and began producing wristwatches in 1931.

In 1936, the company established its Tanashi factory in Tokyo, allowing it to significantly expand its industrial capacity and begin exporting watches to Southeast Asia, China, and elsewhere. By 1941, it established a machine-tool business that allowed it to move beyond watchmaking, and which still exists to this day. Postwar expansion continued at a dizzying rate, with Citizen developing shock-resistant Parashock technology in 1956 (which it demonstrated by dropping watches out of a helicopter.) In 1959, it debuted the Parawater, Japan’s first fully waterproof watch, while in 1962 came the Diamond Flake, an ultra-thin model equipped with a hand-wound movement.

By the 1960s, Citizen began experimenting with technologies that would come to define its place in the contemporary watch market: first, in 1966, came the X-8, the country’s first electronic wristwatch, followed in 1970 by the X-8 Chronometer, which holds the distinction of being the world’s very first titanium wristwatch. And while the brand wasn’t the first to embrace quartz technology, it did so with vigor by 1973, introducing the Crystron Mega in 1975, which was accurate to an astonishing +/- 3 seconds per year.

The technology that would eventually evolve into Eco-Drive—the brand’s signature system that converts ambient light into electrical energy, thus reducing the frequency of battery replacements—debuted in 1976 with the advent of its Crystron Solar Cell, the world’s first light-powered, analog-dialed quartz watch. Then, in 1982, Citizen released the Professional Diver, which boasted an incredible-for-the-era 1,300m of water resistance; it followed up three years later with the Aqualand, the first dive watch with an onboard electronic depth gauge. In 1989, it gathered its diverse sports watch offerings together within the Promaster line.

Innovation continued throughout the 1990s and beyond, with Citizen introducing the world’s first multizone radio-controlled wristwatch in 1993. In 2011, it debuted the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave, a watch that synchronized with satellites to provide hyper-accurate timekeeping across different time zones. Five years later, in 2016, the Eco-Drive One hit the market with a light-powered movement just 1mm thick, building upon ultra-thin offerings from the brand’s back catalog. Then, in 2019, it brought forth the Eco-Drive Caliber 0100, a movement with stunning accuracy of +/- one second per year.

Today, Citizen Group is more than just Citizen: comprising movement maker Miyota; the aforementioned precision machinery business; watch brands Bulova and Frédérique Constant; and many more. It’s a vertically-integrated behemoth with over 12,000 employees worldwide. Founded over a century ago, it’s grown into one of the most important brands within the watchmaking sphere—and beyond.

Like fellow Japanese watchmaker Seiko, Citizen produces such a dizzying array of timepieces that it’s nearly impossible to categorize them in any definitive manner. Many modern collections overlap in remit with older historical collections that are still kicking around, while certain collections are only available in certain markets. (Some frankly defy categorization entirely, and others should probably be cut from the brand’s catalog.)

What follows, therefore, is a helpful (albeit admittedly non-exhaustive) list of Citizen’s most important and recognizable product families, delineated with the understanding that certain watches—including several dedicated ladies’ collections from the Japanese domestic market—will invariably be left out. Still, the following rough descriptions should help orient the new (or seasoned!) collector.

The Rolan Collection is an excellent place to search for a highly affordable, versatile everyday watch. Using Citizen’s signature Eco-Drive technology, each model is easily powered by ambient light and virtually never requires a battery change. While certain references are paired to matching metal bracelets, others come on stylish straps, and all take their aesthetic inspiration from classic midcentury horological tropes. Simple and handsome, the Rolan Collection watches are perfect for someone in search of their first “nice” watch.

The ref. AW2110-06E is everything you need in a dress/everyday watch, and nothing you don’t: measuring 41mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a radially brushed black sector dial with applied indices and a luminous dauphine handset. Paired to a black leather strap with stainless steel hardware and powered by Eco-Drive technology, it’s a timepiece that could conceivably be paired with just about anything. Moreover, at just a hair over $300, it makes a highly affordable gift for a recent graduate who needs a solid timepiece to wear on an interview or to a first job.

Notable Reference: AW2110-06EType: DressDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. J830 Eco-DrivePrice: $296.25MORE INFO HERE

The Garrison Collection contains military-inspired field watches and chronographs in a variety of styles, most of which are widely available in the United States both online and at authorized dealers. Powered by Eco-Drive technology, they’re reliable, good-looking pieces on bracelets or straps that all retail for less than $500, making them—like their counterparts in the dressier Rolan collection—excellent gifting options. For someone who’s after mil-spec looks but who doesn’t want to shell out for a vintage watch, the Garrison is a perfect choice.

The ref. BM8180-03E is ubiquitous at Citizen points of sale and neatly encapsulates the brand’s ethos. Measuring a comfortable 37mm in brushed stainless steel, it features a black dial with luminous Arabic indices, an outer five-minute track, and a day-date display at 3 o’clock. Powered by Eco-Drive, highly legible, and wildly affordable, it still boasts 100m of water resistance and a two-piece green fabric strap for less than $300. Looking like an issued military watch from the 1960s, it’s ideal for outdoor use while hiking or on vacation

Notable Reference: BM8180-03EType: FieldDiameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. E100 Eco-DrivePrice: $221.25MORE INFO HERE

Established in 1989, the Promaster Collection is where Citizen parks its most formidable tool watches. Organized into Marine, Sky, and Land sub-categories, they localize many of the notable innovations that the brand developed over the past 60-plus years, from the early Parawater’s water resistance to the Aqualand’s electronic depth gauge to the Navihawk’s multifunction timekeeping and more. Today, the collection encompasses everything from mechanical divers to radio-controlled pilot’s watches to exploration–oriented field watches.

Though Citizen is famous for its Eco-Drive technology, it’s worth noting that the brand has a long history of crafting hardcore mechanical tool watches. The modern Promaster Fujitsubo ref. NB6026-56L—a limited edition based upon a vintage 1977 diver found still ticking on an Australian beach in 1983—is one such timepiece: crafted from Super Titanium with a platinum DLC coating, it features a marine-blue bezel and dial, a matching triple-link bracelet, 200m of water resistance, and an automatic movement with a power reserve of 42 hours.

Notable Reference: NB6026-56LType: DiverDiameter: 41mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,195MORE INFO HERE

Launched in 2008, discontinued in 2014, and relaunched in 2021, the Series8 Collection is based upon the idea of an angular, eight-part case design containing a mechanical movement. Though the collection has expanded somewhat beyond its original aesthetic remit, it still emphasizes the idea of a contemporary mechanical offering with clean lines and bold geometric forms. Comprising several model families, the Series8 features integrated-style bracelets, textured dials, and enhanced resistance to magnetic fields of up to 16,000 A/m.

Executed a tasteful two-tone colorway, the Series 8 831 ref. NB6059-57P offers outsize value from a variety of angles: there’s its 40mm stainless steel case with surprisingly sophisticated geometry. But there’s also its handsome champagne dial with rose gold-tone furniture; its cal., 9051 automatic movement; its tapered H-link bracelet with brushed and finished surfaces; and its 100m of water resistance. For someone looking for a handsome and versatile everyday watch that can be worn to the office or to the beach, it’s an excellent, relatively affordable option.

Notable Reference: NB6059-57PType: Everyday Diameter: 40mmMovement: Citizen cal. 9051 automaticPrice: $1,125MORE INFO HERE

Dating to 1987, the Attessa Collection focuses on the marriage of advanced materials with technologies such as Eco-Drive, radio-controlled, and GPS timekeeping. Building upon the Citizen's innovations with titanium from 1970’s X-8 Chronometer, it offers lightweight, function-forward watches in a variety of categories. Sporty, practical, and good looking, the collection’s tool watches ditch classically-inspired looks in favor of aesthetics that balance practical functionality with sleek lines, subdued materials, and elaborate case finishing.

The Attesa F950 ref. CC4107-80H encapsulates many of the technologies that make Citizen’s wares stand out in an extremely crowded marketplace: there’s the 43.2mm Super Titanium case in dark grey DLC with recrystallized titanium accents; the Eco-Drive Satellite Wave GPS movement with time-only signal reception in under three seconds; and the multi-function display with world time, chronograph, perpetual calendar, power reserve, and numerous other features. Fueled by exposure to ambient light, it’s a futuristic tool with streaks of ‘70s inspiration.

Notable Reference: CC4107-80HType: Everyday Diameter: 43.2mmMovement: Citizen cal. F950 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,195MORE INFO HERE

While Citizen has several lines that focus on mechanical watchmaking, Tsuyosa is particularly notable for combining this with stainless steel construction; integrated-bracelet styling; colorful dials; and accessible pricing. Rather than emphasizing advanced materials or modern timekeeping tech, it uses classic 20th-century watchmaking as an inspiration, seemingly adopting the idea that “simpler is better.” From pastel-hued 37mm models to small-seconds designs with undulating dial patterns, it’s one of the brand’s most compelling offerings.

The Tsuyosa 37mm is truly “everything you need and nothing you don’t” in wristwatch form: constructed from stainless steel, it features alternating brushed and polished surfaces throughout its multifaceted case and integrated multi-link bracelet, lending it a sophistication that belies its sub-$1,000 pricing. Available in multiple colors, its sunray dial with luminous indices and magnified date window is legible and direct, while its automatic cal. 8210 movement and 50m of water resistance offer more than enough utility for daily wear. A set-and-forget watch.

Notable Reference: NJ0200-50MType: Everyday Diameter: 37mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8210 automaticPrice: $371.25MORE INFO HERE

The Zenshin Collection brings together several compelling features in a single product family: Super Titanium (titanium construction with Duratect surface-hardening treatments); automatic or Eco-Drive movements; angular cases with integrated bracelets inspired by Citizen’s 1980s output; textured dials; sapphire crystals and 100m of water resistance; and relatively accessible pricing. Available in three-hand or chronograph configurations, Zenshin brings a bit more materials science than TSUYOSA but costs much less than comparable ATTESA fare.

Though a 42.4mm chronograph might smack of early-2000s excess, Super Titanium construction and a comfortable integrated bracelet help keep the Zenshin Chrono under control on the wrist. Available in several colorways, the “panda” colorway ref. CA4610-85A with a textured white-silver dial and contrasting black outer tachymeter scale and chronograph registers is particularly attractive and versatile. Powered by Eco-Drive, it features a 24-hour display as well as a date window—and its sub-$600 price is tough to argue with.

Notable Reference: CA4610-85AType: Everyday Diameter: 42.4mmMovement: Citizen cal. B620 Eco-DrivePrice: $506.25MORE INFO HERE

The Citizen is Citizen’s purest expression of horological excellence. Building upon the firm’s legacy of highly accurate mechanical timekeeping in the midcentury Chronomaster Collection, it launched the collection in 1995 to emphasize its commitment to precision, simplicity, and longevity. Today, it comprises both mechanical and Eco-Drive movements, marrying them with traditional Japanese craftsmanship in the form of washi paper dials, hand-dyed indigo treatments, and other decorative techniques. Wildly accurate, they’re among Citizen’s most expensive offerings.

If you’re the type of person who values accuracy above all else in a wristwatch—but wouldn’t mind great looks thrown in for good measure—then chances are The Citizen ref. AQ4100-65X might be the timepiece for you. Measuring 38.3mm in the brand’s Super Titanium, it’s powered by the Eco-Drive cal. A060, which is accurate to ±5 seconds per year. (And that innocuous-seeming date window? It’s actually a perpetual date window that doesn’t need manual updating.) Oh, and the red dial? Ultra-thin Tosa Tengujoshi paper. (Hence the price.)

Notable Reference: AQ4100-65XType: Dress/everyday Diameter: 38.3mmMovement: Citizen cal. A060 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,995MORE INFO HERE

If The Citizen Collection is all about accuracy, then the Eco-Drive One Collection is about combining that accuracy—achieved via the brand’s signature Eco-Drive technology—with mind-bogglingly thin case architecture and precision manufacturing. Launched in 2016 to coincide with the 40th anniversary of Eco-Drive, the lineup features several watches powered by the cal. 8826, which was introduced as the world’s thinnest light-powered at just 1mm tall. When combined with Super Titanium and other advanced tech, these pieces are truly remarkable.

Not only is the Eco-Drive One a spectacularly thin timepiece at just 4.5mm tall—it’s also a remarkably handsome dress watch: measuring 36.6mm wide in stainless steel, it features a flat grey dial with a contrasting dark-grey minute track; applied gold-tone indices with a matching dauphine handset; and a dark-grey sub-seconds display above 6 o’clock. That its crown is tiny shouldn’t be much of a concern—the cal. 8845 Eco-Drive is so accurate that you’ll hardly ever need to adjust it. (Unless you change time zones, that is.) Truly a one-and-done dress watch.

Notable Reference: AQ5010-01AType: DressDiameter: 36.6mmMovement: Citizen cal. 8845 Eco-DrivePrice: $2,395MORE INFO HERE

A limited-edition offering celebrating 50 years of Eco-Drive technology, the Eco-Drive Photon Collection combines highly geometric case shapes with dials inspired by the physics of light transmission. Currently available in two references, each features a slit dial that dynamically shifts in color as light interacts with its surface. Paired with integrated bracelets and measuring 39.6mm in Super Titanium, they’re more premium offerings than many Citizen core-catalog models, but significantly less expensive than Eco-Drive One or The Citizen watches.

A distinctive, contemporary take on the luxury sports watch, the Eco-Drive Photon ref. BJ6569-59X combines dark grey and amber yellow Super Titanium case construction to eye-catching effect. The three-dimensional dial with overlapping slits creates even more visual interest, while the cal. E036 Eco-Drive movement turns any light source into a consistent source of accurate timekeeping. Water resistant to 50m, the Photon is an excellent everyday option for someone who appreciates a bit of Genta-inflected styling in their wardrobe.

Big Walk review bombed after House House admits AI touched “well under 0.1%” of code

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Big Walk, the co-op adventure from Untitled Goose Game developer House House, has been hit by a wave of negative Steam reviews after the studio disclosed that large language models were used during the final stretch of development.

House House added an AI section to Big Walk’s FAQ explaining that chatbot responses were occasionally used as starting points for modified game code. The studio estimates that “well under 0.1%” of the game’s code had anything to do with that usage and says generative AI was not used in any other part of development.

Big Walk launched on August 4 and remains rated Very Positive overall on Steam. However, its review history changed sharply after the disclosure spread. Steam recorded 135 positive reviews and three negative reviews on November 9.

The following day, that shifted to 426 positive and 366 negative reviews. The November 11 figures were 358 positive and 90 negative when checked.

House House said its primary use of LLMs involved researching problems with third-party code. The studio gave one example involving Unity’s Universal Render Pipeline, where shadow casters behind the player were reportedly rendered at an infinite distance.

TLDR on the Big Walk article. Some awards judges say they won’t vote for games that used gen AI during development. House House used a chatbot to help fix bugs. The studio says it didn’t use AI to make the game’s art.The example they gave was a bug in Unity’s graphics system.… https://t.co/yzD2R2nTM2

According to House House, the issue made Big Walk run around 50% slower for approximately 20 seconds during sunrise and sunset scenes. The developers said they investigated the problem themselves, spoke with a Unity engineer and eventually asked a chatbot for another possible solution.

The chatbot suggested a fix that House House then modified extensively. The studio said the final change represented around 10 lines of code inside URP.

House House said it did not disclose the coding assistance on Steam because Valve does not request disclosure of code or chatbot usage. It said the FAQ statement was originally prepared for its Independent Games Festival submission and was published after the matter became public.

Indie game platform itch.io says it deleted “a couple terabytes” of AI slop

Publisher blames “AI-slop” on Steam as their game only gets 15 players at launch

Epic Games offers $50,000 for AI games made in vibe-coding competition

-Big Walk used AI-looks inside-It was used to alter 10 lines of code to fix a bug a human engineer and coder said would be impossible for a human to fix-Never used AI to generate anything-Game and Devs still get hateIf you are one of the dumbasses attacking them, stfu. https://t.co/tdbEwt2hPY

The studio stressed that the statement was not an endorsement of AI tools, apologized that the use might not meet some people’s standards and called the lack of an internal AI policy a failure. House House said it plans to create a more comprehensive policy going forward.

The debate began after 19 IGF jurors said they would decline to vote for any game that used generative AI during development. Their statement included AI-generated code and said it did not speak for the entire IGF organization or every juror.

House House disclosed the AI use as part of its IGF submission, but no official announcement has confirmed that Big Walk was disqualified from the awards.

GTA 6 Lead Says The Series Still Has “So Much To Do” In America

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Rockstar Games co-studio head Rob Nelson says future Grand Theft Auto games could take place outside of America, but that the setting still has “so much to do”.

When Grand Theft Auto 6 launches in just over a week, it’s poised to be one of the most detailed and expansive open-world games of all time. Just like how GTA 5 built on San Andreas with its much bigger take on Los Santos, GTA 6 is transforming Vice City into Leonida, a whole state that’s now one big tribute to Florida instead of just Miami.

Even though Rockstar is primarily based in Scotland and the first two games in the series were set in an alternate version of London, every single GTA since has been set somewhere in America. Fans have wondered for some time whether Rockstar would ever consider going somewhere new, and we finally have an, admittedly vague, answer.

Earlier today, The Guardian published an interview with Rockstar North co-studio head and GTA 6 lead Rob Nelson about the game’s impending release. A big focus of the interview is how much work goes into creating Rockstar’s worlds and making them as believable as possible, with Nelson notably reminiscing about staying out in a Miami nightclub until 4am.

62 items, and you don't need to visit the stores in-person to get them.

After pointing out that Rockstar and GTA are famous for depicting American cities and the US, The Guardian asked Nelson about the possibility of the series having a game set outside of America. In response, Nelson said, “Never say never”, finally giving long-standing GTA Tokyo believers a little bit of hope in the process.

Funnily enough, former Rockstar developer Obbe Vermeij recently suggested that the studio was “definitely” considering letting a Japanese studio develop a GTA game.

That being said, you probably shouldn’t count on Grand Theft Auto returning to London any time soon, as Nelson noted that the team still has “so much to do in America”, even with the series being pushed to its limits with Leonida. Nelson thinks that everyone is an outsider to GTA’s version of America, which is something the team doesn’t seem in a rush to step away from.

Still, it’s about as close as we’ve ever gotten to hearing Rockstar say that Grand Theft Auto could exist outside of the US. I think everyone is expecting the eventual next game to give us a grand return to Liberty City, but here’s hoping that whatever follows that isn’t afraid to leave the States behind.
