In a world full of $200 controllers, this $35 Xbox pad manages to afford anti-drift sticks

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Get the biggest gaming news, reviews, and releases straight to your inbox.

Join the GamesRadar community for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Drakong Challenger seeks to challenge expectations of what a pro controller should be priced at, as it hits the market swinging with features that you wouldn’t find on a first-party controller double its price.

Small and lighter than many other controllers

Why you can trust GamesRadar+ Our experts review games, movies and tech over countless hours, so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about our reviews policy.

Over the last few years, I have found myself gravitating further and further towards PC gaming, but after spending 8 hours at a desk all day at work, I don’t want to spend my downtime sat in front of a mouse and keyboard - I want to be firmly planted on my sofa with a controller in hand. The only downside to this is that the best Xbox Series X controllers that dominate the PC gamepad market can be expensive, so I needed something that offered as many features as possible without the price tag that comes with it. This is where newcomer brand Drakong comes in.

Drakong, a new peripheral brand owned by Hyperkin, seeks to give gamers like me the experience I crave but at a budget price; it strips away the fancy frills like RGB and wireless connectivity to keep the price down, while focusing on providing you with high-performing Hall effect sticks and triggers instead.

At a very reasonable price of $34.99 / £45, the Drakong Challenger certainly keeps its promise as a budget controller. But can the Challenger “challenge” some of its more expensive peers to be worthy of your hard-earned cash?

Like many of the best PC controllers, the Drakong Challenger has taken its design cues from Xbox’s iconic controller. The layout of the sticks is offset as you’d expect for an Xbox pad; it has the classic button layout and trigger position, and has the Xbox home logo front and centre to mark its official licensing from Microsoft. The only visual differences on the surface are the black shell, which features a print of green around the thumbsticks, and the M button, which I’ll elaborate on in a second.

On the back of the controller, the plastic is textured to offer some form of additional grip; jury is still out on how effective that is, in my opinion, but more on that later. Also on the rear of the pad are the back buttons. This is a must-have feature in my mind when it comes to being a pro controller in 2026 - I say that, but it should really be standard across all gaming controllers at this point, looking at you, Sony and Microsoft. Thankfully, mapping these is nice and easy without the use of companion software, because like any of GameSir’s controllers, the ‘M’ button I mentioned a paragraph back will help you tailor them to your needs.

One of the most noticeable aspects of the Drakong Challenger for me is how small it feels. While it visually looks the same as any Xbox controller, it appears to be slightly smaller, which can make or break how it feels in hand for gamers. In addition to this, it is also very light compared to others I’ve tested, like the PowerA Fusion Pro Wireless (645g), coming in at roughly 205g.

I can’t say the Challenger looks or feels “premium,” as it’s dressed in a fairly cheap-feeling plastic. This may be a turn-off to some, but maybe don’t expect too much more for the price.

Hardware nerds unite, sign up to our free tech newsletter for a weekly digest of the hottest new tech, the latest gadgets on the test bench, and much more from the team at GamesRadar+.

The standout inclusion for me is Hall Effect thumbsticks and triggers; especially in a budget option like this, it really speaks to Sony and Microsoft’s homegrown controllers costing more and yet sticking to their stick drift-prone potentiometer modules. Drakong opting for Hall effect in a more affordable alternative shows how easy it is, and will guarantee that you won’t get drift here.

Another key feature that I’ve previously mentioned are the back buttons; they feel nice and responsive and are placed in a really good spot for where my middle fingers rest on the rear of the controller. For something this cheap, it’s nice to see this feature being added.

I also appreciate the fact that the buttons are programmable without software; you simply press the ‘M’ button, and then press the button you want programmed. It vibrates to confirm it has worked, and then you’re good. While it isn’t documented anywhere when unboxing the controller, it’s pretty easy to work out.

Speaking of vibration, when I first picked up the Challenger and felt how light it is, I assumed it wouldn’t feature rumble. But to my surprise, when I programmed one of the back buttons, the whole pad shook in my hand. I feel like this is a feature that would be very easy for them to have cut to keep the price down and one that’s often missing in lighter gamepads, but I am glad it is here.

Given the price point, I was ready for there to be compromises when actually using the Challenger day-to-day, and I really put it through its paces across a number of titles - Assassin’s Creed Black Flag Resynced, Grand Theft Auto V, Resident Evil 2 Remake and The Blood of Dawnwalker. Across all titles, I found that the back buttons - like they are with most controllers - are the standout feature. They really elevate my experience when gaming, and it is no different here. They allowed me to really lock into the experience and gave me ways to tailor the way I play a game. My parries in Black Flag and Dawnwalker really benefited from this feature.

Thanks to the Hall Effect sticks and triggers, all of my inputs felt responsive and precise, especially when I was in the halls of the RCPD, and I needed to conserve ammo and pop heads. I had previously played through this game with my standard Xbox controller and found the experience here to be much more controlled. This extends to Grand Theft Auto V, where the triggers, while very responsive and sensitive, did allow me to very quickly accelerate and brake whenever a civilian decided to get in the path of my car - I’ll let you decide if I was speeding up or slowing down.

Having said that, I really don’t like the texture of the thumbsticks. They’re a soft plastic with a textured rim around the edge, but I found my thumbs slipping off them more often than not. I feel like they don’t provide much grip when using the pad and that’s something that is quite essential for playing games. When they worked and I could keep my thumbs in position, they were great, but I was really disappointed with how cheap this aspect felt.

This extended to the textured grip on the back; while I appreciate that it is trying to make it easy to hold the device, it didn’t stop the cheap plastic from sliding about in my hand. What doesn’t help this is the fact that it feels noticeably smaller in my hand than my go-to controllers - the PS5 DualSense Edge and the Power A Fusion Pro Wireless - so I constantly felt like I was holding a toy in comparison to what I normally use.

Across the rest of the Challenger, I found the face buttons and D-pad to be pretty standard; they provided me with good responses and nice tactile feedback. A nice surprise sitting at the bottom of the controller was the headphone jack; while I wouldn’t normally use headphones that are plugged into a controller, this is a nice addition if you want to plug in a mic.

I’d have liked to have wireless functionality included here, but I understand why it isn’t; they want to keep the cost down, and this is one way to do it. The USB-C cable provided is a good length and works, but for anyone whose PC is further away from their sofa and TV, it may be a little bit of a struggle to use outside of a desk environment.

I think the big selling point here is the fact that the Drakong Challenger isn’t going to break the bank; it’s $25 cheaper than the official Xbox controller, which is a whole portion of Avocado Toast in the current economic climate. For the price you are paying, you are getting a really good budget option that opens you up to some pro controller features.

For half the price, you’re getting Hall-effect sticks and triggers, and rear programmable buttons; unless you wanted something wireless, then for me it would be a no-brainer between those two choices. It’s really hard to beat the Challenger on a price-to-performance scale.

While it won’t be the controller I decide to use in my daily gaming life due to it feeling too small and light in my hands, plus finding the thumbsticks a bit uncomfortable to use, I can recognise that this would be a great purchase for anyone looking for a new controller… or has smaller hands.

I used the controller for a few weeks as my primary controller when playing games on my PC. I tested Assassin’s Creed Black Flag Resynced, Grand Theft Auto V, Resident Evil 2 Remake, and The Blood of Dawnwalker while using the controller.

For more on how we test controllers, take a look at the GamesRadar+ hardware policy.

The European Commission's new Digital Fairness Act could change monetisation in games forever - and not just in Europe

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"Well, we'll just do this for the rest of the world."

The European Commission has published its proposed Digital Fairness Act, which has the potential to change how video games are monetised - worldwide - forever. A draft of the Act was leaked yesterday and what's been published today appears to be, to my eye, the same, though there's now also a mini-site accompanying it that explores the Act in more detail and uses more understandable language.

And note that while the Digital Fairness Act isn't about video games exclusively - it's much broader than that - it does mention video games specifically, highlighting both their increased importance to society now, and that policy-shaping bodies like the European Commission are prepared to take them seriously.

The Digital Fairness Act is set to become European law, probably sometime between 2028 and 2029, once everyone's discussed it and made whatever amendments they're going to make to it. And at that point, breaching the regulations will come with potentially steep fines (up to six percent of a company's worldwide annual turnover, or up to €2m where an annual turnover isn't available).

The biggest things in the Digital Fairness Act relating to video games are regulations around loot boxes (sometimes referred to as "variable reward systems" or systems with "uncertainty-based rewards") and virtual currencies, particularly with regard to "minors" - anyone under 18 years old.

The Act stipulates paid-for virtual currencies must be disabled in a game by default for minors, then activated by parental and guardian approval, and that paid loot boxes "shall not" be offered to minors at all. Moreover, games must provide a way for everyone to disable paid loot boxes completely, and clearly show the probability of receiving items from them.

It's these changes that have the potential to overhaul how loot boxes work everywhere going forwards, and whether companies use them at all. To understand why, I talked to games business expert George Osborn, who runs the excellent Video Games Industry Memo newsletter, and who's been following the Act closely.

Osborn sees this as a "really big" regulatory change and believes that companies like EA, Xbox, Activision Blizzard, PlayStation, and other big gaming companies, both in console gaming and mobile gaming, will be "really concerned" by this. That's partly because a lot of these things being outlined are not necessarily easy to implement.

For starters, a company now needs a way to work out who's a minor playing its game and who isn't, and it needs to provide "a master switch", as Osborn calls it, for people to switch paid loot boxes off with. These things require not just design changes but perhaps entire alterations to a game's approach; it means theoretically being able to turn paid card packs off in EA Sports FC Ultimate Team, example, and their not being able to be sold to minors at all.

"EA might be looking at it and going, 'This isn't great news for [EA Sports FC] Ultimate Team because people can switch it off,'" Osborn added, "but Ultimate Team is at least hived off in a box." EA could potentially age-gate Ultimate team as a mode and deal with it that way. But what if you're a card game where random paid rewards are central to your premise? "What that now means is you're possibly going to have to find a way to just completely turn that off for users, and it is very difficult to do that when they're embedded into the design of the game."

The virtual currency issue is potentially easier to tackle because existing parental controls can control spending, so those tools could expand to cover it. But another proposal brought forward in the Digital Fairness Act states that a game must clearly show, in a region's local currency, how much an item costs when bought with virtual currency, which adds another layer of complication when considering different currencies and people moving between countries with their devices.

These changes require investment and thought, and they sit alongside broader changes that affect things like subscription services, such as Xbox Live and PlayStation Plus and Nintendo Online, to make them easier to cancel and less able to obfuscate prices and hidden extras. There's even a stipulation that people be able to talk to a human in customer service rather than a chat bot. There are more technicalities for game-making companies to consider.

But why should people outside of the European Union care? These rules are only enforceable there. Because: if a company is selling a game into the European Union, which I expect every game company will want to do - "you don't just want to rule out a market of a few hundred million consumers; it's not a light thing to cut that out", Osborn says - then it will have to comply by these rules. And if it does, how many different versions of a game will it be prepared to make?

Think about the recent global move towards all devices using USB-C chargers: that was a movement started by a European Commission ruling. Think of the new Switch 2s with removable battery packs: that was another change instigated by the European Commission. "Because that market is big enough, you design for it," Obsorn says, "and then your logic becomes, 'Well, we'll just do this for the rest of the world.' You're not going to produce one with the battery sealed in and the battery without. You're just going to find one way to produce things.

"That's likely to be the biggest impact in the UK and the rest of the world," he adds, "as people adjust the designs of their games to follow these rules. There's going to be a strong likelihood that some of the design features will remain within the games because it's easiest to work off a single universal version."

The other thing, which Osborn thinks is more important, is that policy makers around the world are "desperate" for solutions to problems and don't know where to look for them. "So if someone comes up with an answer to a question that's been annoying everybody, like 'what do we do about loot boxes?'," he says, "and it provides a particularly strong - or perceived to be effective - solution, it'll be copied. And it'll be copied because a big regulatory bloc like the European Union, which is known for protecting consumers particularly well, has decided this is a good thing. People trust it."

Then, because companies have already developed a way to comply with those rules, it becomes difficult for them to argue that they can't do it elsewhere. "Because those regulators go, 'Well why not?'" Osborn says. "Because if you build the solution in one place, you can build it elsewhere."

Whether or not this will be the end of loot boxes as we know them, Osborn isn't sure. "My guess would be this will continue to push loot boxes into the fringes of video game monetisation. It was already starting to happen," he says. "I know some really high-profile games have them very prominently, which makes people feel like is there really any change here? But I know a lot of companies behind the scenes started to quietly move away from them in the last few years when they were a reputational problem. If you move to a world in which you're meant to have a massive off-switch in your game for them, that's a world in which fewer companies will go in that direction."

Some will stick with it. "If you're an EA, it's going to be very difficult to move away from the model that has supported your business for years," Osborn says. And card-based deck-building games will struggle to avoid it. "But overall, we're going to see a shift away from it," he adds. "And more broadly what this marks is a much wider crackdown on the way that video games monetise their players. As you start to see that crackdown come into force, game developers are going to have to think much more carefully about how the design decisions they make don't just interact with ethical practices, but actually with legal practices too."

XRP Price Tests $1 Support Amid David Schwartz’s Admission That He Has Stopped Buying XRP

David Schwartz, Ripple’s former chief technology officer, says he has not bought XRP in a long time and has sold nearly all the digital assets he has ever owned. His comments come as XRP extends its decline, trading near $1.06 after a 17.2% drop over the past week.

“I haven’t bought XRP in a long time,” Schwartz wrote on X. “I am very uncomfortable with risk and have sold nearly all of the digital assets I’ve ever held.”

Schwartz has cut his crypto holdings from their peak:

Schwartz said he still holds “quite a bit” of Ripple stock, and that this alone carries “more risk than I wish I had.” He has said in the past that he “really, really hates risk.” Since around 2012, he has followed a rule of selling some of his crypto each time it hits a new high.

The disclosure follows his recent post saying he paid about $1.7 million in capital gains tax after selling around 2.5 million XRP in 2025.

XRP is trading near $1.06, according to market data. It is down 2.5% in 24 hours and 17.2% over the past seven days, among the worst performers in the top 10 cryptocurrencies.

According to an analyst, the token remains in a falling channel and downside momentum is still strong. XRP has now dropped below $1.10, the 78.6% Fibonacci retracement and a level bulls had hoped would hold.

The same outlook expects XRP to keep tracking Bitcoin. If Bitcoin falls further, XRP is likely to follow, since altcoins often drop harder than Bitcoin during corrections.

The outlook says XRP could still form a low in this area, because the decline so far looks like a correction and not the start of a new downtrend. If XRP fails to react in the coming weeks, though, recovery will get “really, really difficult” for buyers. A confirmed bottom would need a strong five-leg rally above the first resistance.

A proposed tabletop quantum sensor could match the precision of the best satellite measurements of frame dragging, a subtle effect predicted by Einstein's general relativity.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Scientists have proposed a new tabletop device to detect the subtle way Earth's daily spin on its axis twists the space-time around it ‪—‬ an effect predicted by Einstein's general relativity.

If built, the sensor could measure the quantum effect known as frame dragging with the same precision as the best satellite experiments, but in minutes instead of years, the researchers proposed in a new study.

The research, published Sept. 17 in the journal Physical Review Letters, aimed to address a classic mismatch in physics. Picture a ring of clocks bolted to a spinning merry-go-round. You synchronize each clock with its neighbor around the ring. Then, you compare the last clock with the first. They don't match.

"It is impossible to synchronize that collection of clocks when you're in a rotating frame," Kai-Isaak Ellers, a physics graduate student at the University of California, Berkeley (UC Berkeley) and first author of the study, told Live Science.

A photo of scientists working on NASA's Gravity Probe B in 2003. This device measured Earth's frame-dragging from space, confirming Einstein's predictions with an 18% margin of error.

Einstein's theory of general relativity predicts that a massive, spinning object drags the fabric of space-time along with it, just like a spoon twirling in honey. The effect is clearly visible around supermassive objects, like the Milky Way's central black hole. But around Earth, the effect is very faint.

So far, scientists have measured Earth's frame dragging only from orbit. They tracked how the twisting of space-time slowly shifts spinning gyroscopes or the paths of satellites. In 2011, NASA's Gravity Probe B satellite, which carried four gyroscopes, confirmed Einstein's prediction with a margin of error of about 18%. Satellites tracked with lasers have since done even better. In July, researchers reported in the journal Nature that the LARES-2 satellite had shrunk that margin of error to just 0.2%.

However, that took about three years of laser tracking. Researchers also had to account for the fact that Earth bulges at the equator and its mass is spread unevenly, which gives the planet’s gravity small irregularities that tug on satellites far more strongly than frame dragging does. To subtract those tugs, the team relied on detailed maps of Earth's gravity made by other satellites.

Measuring frame dragging more precisely is a way to stress-test general relativity. Several alternative theories of gravity predict a slightly different amount of twisting, and a lab-based measurement would also be free of the modeling assumptions that satellite experiments depend on, Ellers said.

The new device is a loop of superfluid helium-4 — helium chilled until it flows without friction and behaves like a single, giant quantum object. The loop is interrupted by a thin barrier riddled with tiny holes, called a Josephson junction. Superfluid helium can slip through the holes, but ordinary liquid helium cannot.

"That's where the quantum behavior comes from," said K. Birgitta Whaley, a quantum physicist at UC Berkeley and a co-author of the study.

As Earth spins and drags space-time, it nudges the quantum state of the helium flowing through the junction. That nudge shifts the frequency at which the helium sloshes back and forth in the loop, which the researchers could detect.

A visualization of Earth's uneven gravity field taken by NASA's Gravity Recovery and Climate Experiment in 2009. The lumpiness of our planet's gravitational field makes it harder to precisely measure relativistic effects.

Superfluid devices, many of them built at UC Berkeley, have detected Earth's rotation since the late 1990s. But the helium-4 versions built around a Josephson junction, like the new design, have worked within about a hundredth of a degree of 2.17 K (minus 455.76 Fahrenheit, or minus 270.98 Celsius), the temperature at which helium-4 becomes a superfluid. At that temperature, a lingering fraction of "normal" helium mixed in with the superfluid adds noise.

Instead, the team calculated how the device would behave at a few thousandths of a degree above absolute zero. Below 0.7 K, the noise changed character, and the team found it drops sharply as the helium gets colder. One key finding in the new paper is the result of the team's thermal noise analysis, which showed how sensitive these devices could be at millikelvin temperatures, Elles said.

The catch is the barrier. Earlier helium-4 devices used holes about 90 nanometers wide. Working at these frigid temperatures demands much smaller holes, about 1 nanometer across ‪—‬ roughly the size of a helium atom. Keith Schwab, a physicist at Caltech and co-author of the study, and his collaborators are developing such materials, but they haven't been built into a working sensor yet.

If they can be, the researchers calculate that the device could measure frame dragging to 0.2% precision in about 35 minutes. If its vibrating membrane were made from the lowest-loss crystals, such as quartz or sapphire, that could drop to about one second, they said.

"This seems to be a very serendipitous discovery that at millikelvin, we can get this high sensitivity," Whaley told Live Science.

The signal corresponds to a time difference of about 10-35 seconds between helium flowing one way around the loop and the other — the same mismatch as the clocks on the metaphorical merry-go-round.

"The interesting thing about it is just how small it is," Whaley said. "It's not so far off from the Planck time," about 10-44 seconds, the shortest slice of time before the known laws of physics are expected to break down. This experiment is still a billion times too large to reach that limit, Whaley said. But future helium experiments could help explore whether time flows smoothly, like a ramp, or ticks forward in tiny, indivisible jumps, like the frames of a movie.

Ellers, K.-I., Christodoulou, M., Schwab, K. C., & Whaley, K. B. (2026). Quantum sensing of gravitational frame dragging with a superfluid ⁴He gyrometer. Physical Review Letters, 137(12), 121404. https://doi.org/10.1103/q3v9-rjm9

She obtained a B.S. in physics and a B.A. in English creative writing in 2024, which enabled her to pursue a career that integrates both disciplines. She reports mainly on environmental, Earth and physical sciences, but is always willing to write about any science that sparks her curiosity. Her work has appeared in Eos, Science News, Space.com, among others.

Another day, another cybersecurity update. This time, it's the turn of immensely popular NAS provider Synology to advise its users about some now-fixed gaping holes. The company has updated its DiskStation Manager (DSM) operating system, patching out two security vulnerabilities in various product versions.

Just another patch? Not quite. The first of these vulnerabilities has a CVSS3 base score of 9.8 out of 10, which makes it of "critical" severity (via International Cyber Digest on X). According to the company:

"An improper neutralization of special elements used in an SQL command vulnerability in the Auth API in Synology DiskStation Manager (DSM)... allows remote attackers to read or write arbitrary files and conduct denial-of-service attacks."

That's a pretty big security hole if I've ever seen one, particularly for those storing extremely important data on their home NAS setup. The second one gets a lower severity rating of 8.6, but also manages to allow attackers to skip the login process.

Synology describes the second vulnerability as "An authentication bypass using an alternate path or channel vulnerability in the rsync in Synology DiskStation Manager," which can again lead to unwanted reads, writes, and DDOS shenanigans.

‼️BREAKING: A critical flaw in Synology NAS devices lets attackers read and write any file on them without logging in. The SQL injection bug in their login system scores 9.8 out of 10. A second flaw, in rsync, skips the login too. Synology patched both today. Update ASAP. https://t.co/LlS328SBf4November 11, 2026

The company also notified users late last month of ransomware attacks related to a 2024 security advisory, for what looks to be a series of separate vulnerabilities. One way or the other, the latest updates look to have rolled out to many users already, but it's still worth checking to make sure you're on the newest builds to plug all these leaks.

After all, auto updaters are far from perfect. 2026 has been a year characterised by cybersecurity issues, and it'd really put the tin lid on it if your home NAS was broken into by some unscrupulous character. A reminder to keep on top of your updates no matter the device, methinks. Stay safe out there, folks.

Stablecoin supply drops $14.2 billion in largest contraction since 2022 bear market

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Looking for institutional-grade crypto trading? Discover LMAX Digital, the regulated exchange from LMAX Group

USDT and USDC shed nearly $11 billion in supply over the past month as analysts flag less buying power for a crypto rebound.

Crypto's pool of stablecoins is shrinking at its fastest pace in more than four years, alongside a stalled market recovery

A Wednesday report from Glassnode found that the supply of major stablecoins fell by $14.2 billion over the past 30 days, the biggest drop since the 2022 crypto bear market. For comparison, the 30-day stablecoin supply decline nearly reached $30 billion in June 2022, roughly five months before bitcoin reached its bear market low of $15,500 that November.

The total stablecoin supply now sits just below $280 billion, levels not seen since September 2025.

"A shrinking supply means holders are redeeming stablecoins for dollars and taking that cash out of the market, which leaves less fuel for a rally," Glassnode wrote.

A separate report from CryptoQuant on Tuesday focused on the deteriorating supply of Tether's USDT, the long-time stablecoin market leader. Its market capitalization fell $6.6 billion over the 60 days through Nov. 7, more than twice the $3.2 billion drop over a similar period in December 2022 following the collapse of the FTX exchange.

USDT currently accounts for roughly 62.8% of stablecoin supply, followed by Circle's USDC at 25.1%, according to The Block's data.

Gold-backed tokens have held steady by comparison, with a combined market cap of $5.61 billion. That remains below February's $6.31 billion peak when gold was at an all-time high of around $5,350 per troy ounce.

Glassnode said that a return to stablecoin supply growth would be the "first sign that new money is coming in." Meanwhile, CryptoQuant said a local bitcoin bottom could form before USDT supply flips back to positive territory.

Honda Recalls 636K Pilots, Acura MDXs and TLXs over Faulty Power Steering

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The issue, which took years to diagnose, may cause an unintended arm workout in the middle of a drive.

Honda is recalling 635,808 vehicles over a software issue that could cause a power steering defect. Documents filed with the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) show that the issue was first reported in November 2021 but that Honda wasn’t able to confirm the cause of the malfunction until years later.

The recall includes 2021–2025 Acura TLX models, 2022–2024 Acura MDX models, and 2023–2025 Honda Pilot SUVs (the 2023 Pilot is pictured above). Honda found that a faulty software configuration could cause the electronic power steering (EPS) system to enter a failsafe mode under certain conditions when the vehicle is driving straight.

See all results for used 2025 Honda Pilot for sale near 36265

Under the failsafe mode, power steering is disabled, making the vehicle much harder to steer, and an EPS warning light appears in the instrument cluster. Honda was unable to replicate and name the issue until December 2025 due to dealers and suppliers deleting the error codes as part of their engine diagnostic process when affected cars were serviced.

Honda has received one injury report related to the defect, as well as 146 warranty claims, as of October 22. Owners of affected vehicles will receive notice in the mail by December 14, and should schedule an appointment with an authorized Honda or Acura dealer for a software update at no cost. In the meantime, owners can check the NHTSA recall website to see if their vehicle is involved.

Nvidia board’s idolatry of Jensen Huang could open the door to shareholder lawsuits in Delaware Chancery Court

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

There is no doubt that Nvidia founder and CEO Jensen Huang is a visionary genius, whose vision as the ‘Godfather of AI’ has improved billions of lives around the world and contributed greatly to American prosperity and innovation. He has attended our Yale CEO forums and has been revered by CEOs across sectors and around the world for his bold, entrepreneurial vision and his revolutionary technological triumphs, plus he is a showcased contributor to our new, upcoming book, When Machines Act: The Promise and Perils of our Agentic AI Future, published by MIT Press and written with Two Sigma Co-Founder David Siegel.

Yet, amid calls for Huang’s immediate resignation from top GOP voices, including Sen. Kevin Cramer of the Senate Banking Committee and others, and demands that he answer to Congress, there were some blatant red flags in Huang’s recent leadership that were apparently missed by the Nvidia board.

In October 2025, the New York Times reported that Megaspeed, a little-known Singapore firm, had bought billions of dollars of Nvidia chips amid suspicion that it was helping China evade U.S. restrictions by laundering banned chips on the U.S. export restriction list to China via neutral countries. Nvidia said a review found no evidence its chips were diverted, but that reporting was followed by an explosive report by a short seller, Culper Research, alleging “uncomfortably close ties” between Alice Huang, the head of Megaspeed, and Jensen Huang, in May 2026. Jensen described the Megaspeed dealings as normal, and apparently the Nvidia board was content to leave it at that.

Late last month, the Times reported that U.S. intelligence believed that Alice Huang was a Chinese asset. A company spokesperson said the diversion claims had been debunked and described the relationship as nothing unusual. Ten days later, Jensen admitted he had been unfaithful to his wife, in a message that referred to the national security concerns in the press – a troubling sequence of events after all the repeated denials.

The Caremark doctrine, established in 1996 in Delaware Chancery Court, establishes that corporate directors have a fiduciary duty of oversight, and that directors breach their duty of loyalty when they make no good-faith effort to monitor the risks central to the business, or when they consciously ignore red flags.

Under this doctrine, and derivative variants of it, several boards have run into trouble in Delaware Chancery Court for similar sins of omission when it comes to oversight. In 2019, Delaware’s Supreme Court revived a suit against the directors of Blue Bell Creameries, an ice cream maker whose board had no system for overseeing food safety before a deadly listeria outbreak. In 2021, the Court of Chancery let claims proceed against Boeing’s directors, who had no board committee responsible for airplane safety before the 737 MAX crashes. That case settled for $237.5 million. The lesson of both: on risks that can sink the company, the board must watch for itself: the board cannot simply trust management, even when those risks were unforeseeable or unanticipated.

So plaintiffs’ lawyers will ask the questions directors should have asked many months ago. Who conducted the review that cleared Megaspeed? Did it report to independent directors or to management? Did any director know the CEO had a personal relationship with an executive of the customer under review? Did the board ever discuss Megaspeed at all, or Jensen Huang’s relationship with Alice Huang? Did any board members even care to ask any questions about these topics?

The answers are in the board meeting minutes, and alarmingly for Nvidia’s board, shareholders can now get those minutes with nearly complete transparency. Nvidia knows this better than most companies for a very ironic reason. In 2022, it fought a books-and-records demand all the way to Delaware’s Supreme Court. Even after Delaware narrowed those inspection rights last year, board minutes and board materials remain within reach for plaintiffs litigation. That is how the Boeing plaintiffs built their case.

All of the major risks that have now landed on Nvidia in such dramatic fashion were exactly the risks directors should have been most alert to.

First, and most importantly, every board entrusted with protecting minority shareholders should be alert to management conflicts of interest. A Megaspeed affiliate bought about $4.8 billion of Nvidia-powered equipment in under two years. A chief executive in a personal relationship with an executive of a customer that size has an apparent conflict that Nvidia’s own code of conduct would have likely required him to disclose. And Megaspeed was no ordinary customer: by October 2025 both Washington and Singapore were investigating it.

That conflict poisons the compliance record. When the questions first surfaced, Nvidia said it had visited Megaspeed’s sites and had no reason to believe chips were diverted. That assurance came from people who report to a CEO with a personal tie to the customer being cleared, a tie no investor had been told about. Bloomberg reports that Nvidia’s general counsel went so far as to contact a senior Commerce Department official about the government’s Megaspeed probe. Did the board authorize that call? Did it even know?

Then comes national security and export-control evasion, as “mission critical” a risk as food safety at an ice cream maker or flight safety at Boeing. Nvidia makes the most strategically sensitive product on earth; and The Information reported today that companies tied to Alice Huang helped Chinese buyers obtain $7.5 billion of Nvidia chips, including banned ones that were laundered through neutral parties. That report is unconfirmed, and Megaspeed denies wrongdoing. But the penalties for export violations run from fines to criminal prosecution to the loss of export privileges, and a board should not have been waiting for verdicts before it starts asking questions. According to Nvidia’s own proxy statement, the board committee responsible for trade compliance met three times last fiscal year, and the company has no chief compliance officer. How much did they know, and when?

The national security risk is larger still. According to the Times, U.S. officials concluded that Alice Huang was recruited by China’s Ministry of State Security to get close to Jensen and to shape his views on chip sales to China – which of course, Jensen Huang has been very vocal on in Washington and in the media, arguing that the U.S. should continue to sell chips to China so that China is not building their own chips. No one has alleged that Jensen knew Huang was a spy, and Beijing calls the claims a smear. But the president was reportedly briefed in late 2025, and a secret memo circulated among policymakers this spring, the same year Jensen was appointed to the President’s Council of Advisors on Science and Technology. Now three House committee chairmen want him in a closed-door briefing, and the president says the FBI is reviewing the matter. Yet not one Nvidia director, as Semafor noted, has a national security background; what questions were asked and what prudent diligence steps were taken by the board?

Then there is the risk every board dreads: that management did not tell it the truth. On Oct. 29, a company spokesman described Jensen’s interactions with Alice Huang as “not unusual or unexpected,” the routine customer contact of any CEO. The same week, Jensen called the Megaspeed dealings normal. Nine days after that he told employees he had been unfaithful to his wife, and tied the admission to the national security concerns in the press. If directors approved those October statements knowing what Jensen knew, they could be in trouble. If they never asked him, that is the Caremark problem.

Shareholders have already paid. Nvidia closed today at $168.89, down almost 10% in the two sessions since the admission, a loss of more than $400 billion in market value, and about 30% below its early-October record. Nobody can attribute this recent fall merely to just the AI sell-off, as the evidence shows it was an idiosyncratic move. On Monday, as Nvidia fell 4.3%, the semiconductor index jumped nearly 7%, and direct competitors all rose, including AMD.

The last risk is one the board manufactured itself. Jensen has more than 50 direct reports and no identified successor, and one prominent analyst estimates Nvidia would be worth 25% to 30% less without him. That reflects poorly on the board. Succession is a director’s first duty, and these directors let one man become the company with no obvious succession plan until it is too late.

Even now, the reflex appears to be deference. The same statement that launched the board’s investigation declared, before a single interview, that the board had “no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised,” and that it had confidence in Jensen. A board that announces its conclusion on the day it opens the inquiry, makes one wonder whether the board has already predetermined what their preferred outcome would be rather than following the trail of wherever facts lead them in a way that protects all shareholders.

Readers of Fortune will know that we have been critics of Vice Chancellor Travis Laster on the Delaware Court of Chancery, who wrongly attacked us in his Cloudflare opinion as “advocates for dual class shares” and for founder control more broadly. Vice Chancellor Laster did not apparently understand that we are not advocates for anything; rather, we call it as we see it as fact-based academics with five decades’ experience as a widely recognized scholar of corporate governance.

We have published extensively on dual-class shares, on when they work, and when they don’t, with extensive academic analysis and quantitative research into performance of companies with dual class share structures.

In our extensive careful research, published widely in academic outlets such as the Harvard Law School Forum on Corporate Governance as well as FORTUNE, Chief Executive Magazine and many other outlets, we have studied instances when dual class shares work well, such as when a founder or family with real money at stake uses the protection to invest through cycles and outlast short-term activists, as at Berkshire Hathaway, Alphabet and The New York Times.

Similarly, we have studied instances when dual class shares should be retired, such as when they shield controllers with ulterior motives from consequence, as at WeWork under Adam Neumann or Paramount under the Redstones.

Critics of founder control tend to treat dual-class shares as the bogeyman behind every entrenched founder, and one share, one vote as the cure. Nvidia is the case study that breaks that outdated, prosaic theory. Nvidia has a single class of common stock, not dual class shares. Jensen owns about 3.5% of it and casts 3.5% of the votes. The board is declassified, every director stands for election every year, and a simple majority of directors could replace him as CEO at any meeting. On paper he is as removable as any hired manager. In practice, no CEO in America has been more immune from proper scrutiny and oversight.

What entrenched Jensen was not the capital structure of the company; rather, it was the apparent idolatry of a board which was in thrall socially and interpersonally to Jensen Huang. He earned the adulation honestly, building a company from a booth at Denny’s into, for a time, the most valuable on earth. But the directors whose job it was to provide effective oversight to guard shareholders’ interests, apparently effectively joined the congregation. No charter provision did that, and no proxy adviser would have caught it. Nvidia has the independent majority, the lead director, the annual elections and the single share class that the checklists of ISS and Glass Lewis reward.

Nor has one share, one vote ever been a vaccine against scandal. Enron, Tyco, Wells Fargo and Boeing all had it. One of us argued in the Harvard Business Review in 2002, after Enron, that failed boards routinely passed every structural test, and that what set great boards apart was a culture of open dissent, in which directors challenge the chief executive and each other. That was true then and Nvidia shows it is true now.

When it comes to providing effective oversight, what matters is the oversight culture of the board and the quality of the board, not the ‘check the boxes’ yardsticks so often misleading propagated by governistas, such as ‘one share one vote’.

Delaware needs no new invented legal theory for measuring whether Nvidia’s board did its duty as a board in providing oversight. If shareholders bring Caremark claims against Nvidia’s directors, the court will ask what the board knew and what it did to find out. We have called out the Delaware Court of Chancery when it overreaches, and we will be again. But failing to question a celebrated CEO about a customer under federal investigation is no business judgment at all. Nvidia’s directors were paid to provide oversight over Jensen Huang, however brilliant. And in that duty, by all outside appearances, they apparently whiffed.

Aristotle famously warned, “There is no great genius without a touch of madness.” Psychiatrists call shared disorders “folie à deux.” It is up to the board to ensure a company’s owners benefit from the genius of founders but are protected by the madness.

Steven Tian is the director of research at the Yale Chief Executive Leadership Institute.

XRP’s confidence problem — Why did David Schwartz sell if whales are buying?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

David Schwartz, the co-creator of the XRP Ledger, confirmed on 11th November that his XRP holdings fell from roughly 26 million to about 3 million. When describing his intentions behind the move, he clarified,

I wish I was more comfortable with risk, but I’m just not that person.

The timing of this announcement landed just as XRP lost the $1.15 support zone, leaving the altcoin to trade near $1.05 at press time.

XRP’s RSI had a reading of 32, highlighting the altcoin’s weak momentum. This also implied it wasn’t exactly oversold. $1.00 – $1.03 is the next demand zone, but sustained crashing of the price will open up $0.85 – $0.88.

CoinGecko’s data showed that the market cap fell from $81 billion on 3rd November to $61 billion on 10th November — a $20 billion wipeout in a week. At press time, though, the market cap had risen slightly to $67.22 billion.

The risk-adjusted picture seemed to be just as concerning. According to CryptoQuant data (via ArabxChain), XRP’s Sharpe-like ratio on Binance plummeted to roughly -0.32 as of 10th November, hitting a multi-month low.

A negative Sharpe-like ratio means that the returns XRP generated didn’t even compensate investors. Instead, they bore the risk for no reason while the price cratered from $1.25 to $1.04.

Now, here’s where it gets interesting — On 11th November, analyst Ali Martinez showed that XRP whales continued to accumulate 65.32 million tokens over four days, thereby pushing their holdings to 4.00 billion.

AMBCrypto previously reported that wallets holding at least 1 million XRP bounced to 2,147, a 2026 high, even as the market cap fell by 29%.

Unfortunately for XRP, wallet balance changes don’t prove intent. There is ultimately no proof that whales are accumulating to sell.

Santiment underlined this notion, observing that the growth during price weakness merely “suggests conviction.” So, for now, the 1M+ wallet count is the metric to track. If it keeps climbing without a price hike, XRP’s bull case will get concerning.

XRP is simply caught between two clashing signals. The first is the insider who knows the ledger best and holds almost none of the altcoin. The second group are the largest players in the game, who are persistent in their buying.

The chart presently favors the former CTO’s caution, not the whales’ conviction.

Oura Ring users may get in-app access to real doctors soon – what we know

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Oura's acquisition of Counsel Health could signal a new dawn for digital care.

Oura has entered into an agreement to acquire AI primary care company Counsel Health for $625 million. The acquisition of Counsel Health, which was already a partner in the Oura app, will give users access to Counsel’s in-house physicians and 24/7 medical AI.

The Oura Ring has evolved from its beginnings as a sleep tracker to what its CEO, Tom Hale, calls “a doctor in your pocket.” The company has its sights set on making the device a continuous medical companion, and Counsel’s acquisition is one way it’s furthering that mission.

Oura employs its 24/7 health monitoring to recognize potential deviations from a user’s biometric baseline, and the partnership will build on those capabilities by adding more clinical guidance through Counsel Health.

The partnership will also connect members with Counsel Health’s medical AI and in-house physicians to address timely health problems, support, or “prescriptions for episodic health conditions,” the press release said. The acquisition offers specialty and chronic care for chronic conditions, like reproductive, aging, and cardiometabolic-related conditions.

“Oura tells members what’s happening in their bodies, and Counsel helps them act on it. This combination expands our addressable market and deepens the value we deliver to every member,” David Shuman, executive chairman of Oura, said in a press release.

This is the third acquisition Oura has made this year, following its acquisition of Galen AI, a conversational and personal health platform that connects smart ring data to medical records, and Doublepoint AI, an AI‑driven, biometric and gesture recognition company that could add voice or gesture controls to Oura’s next smart ring.

Also: Before you buy a smart ring, consider what you’re giving up

The Counsel Health deal is expected to close by the end of the year. It’s unknown whether access to Counsel Health physicians or the 24/7 medical AI will be included in an Oura app subscription.

Tech companies have a growing appetite for health tech startups. Last year, Samsung announced a similar acquisition of Xealth, a care platform that connects more than 500 hospitals with 70 digital health centers. The acquisition, according to Samsung VP of digital health Praveen Raja, furthers Samsung’s mission to connect the healthcare landscape, from symptom detection to care provider connection.

One of the Oura Ring’s superpowers is its ability to detect developing illnesses or deviations from my biometric baseline (a spiked resting heart rate, a higher body temperature) before I feel those symptoms. I’ve written about how my Oura Ring detected an illness earlier this year, one day before I began to feel sick.

Also: I should’ve listened to my Oura Ring when it warned me about my health

When Oura has flagged these concerns to me, I take them seriously, even as my care provider — to whom I show this data — may shrug them off. The Counsel Health acquisition enables medical AI and in-house physicians to contextualize your biometrics when you are sick or managing a chronic condition.

Will this acquisition help ease the demand for urgent care clinics? As a regular visitor to and hater of my Brooklyn CityMD, I hope so. It is notoriously difficult to get a same-day, in-person appointment with a primary care provider in New York because of high demand and a stretched-thin healthcare system, which often leaves urgent care as the only in-person option for patients.

The press release says that users could ask physicians for prescriptions. I’m thinking that if I get another bad cold this winter, I could go to my Oura app for help and symptom management instead of CityMD. Making a physician readily available to Oura users could, in theory, reduce dependence on primary or urgent care providers.

Housing demand shows signs of stabilizing. Does your market?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Housing demand may be stabilizing nationally, but the housing market looks different from one community to the next. In Florida's Deltona–Daytona Beach–Ormond Beach metro area, new pending listings rose 20.2% year over year in October 2026, even as some neighboring cities experienced declines. The divergence shows why local housing market trends, including buyer activity, new listings and pricing conditions, matter more than regional averages alone when evaluating market momentum.  AI Summary

Lower mortgage rates are helping stabilize housing demand even as inventory has declined, according to Logan Mohtashami’s latest Housing Market Tracker.

But a national housing demand signal doesn’t mean every local market is following. And even within the same metro area, buyer activity can tell very different stories.

The Deltona–Daytona Beach–Ormond Beach metro area in Florida offers a useful example.

New pending listings across the metro area increased 20.2% year over year in October, according to HousingWire Data. A closer look at individual cities, however, reveals that the improvement was far from uniform.

In Ormond Beach, Fla., new pending listings increased 116.7% year over year in October, rising from 72 in 2025 to 156 in 2026.

Nearby Port Orange recorded an 80% increase, while Daytona Beach saw a 63.2% gain.

But Deltona, one of the cities that gives the metro area its name, experienced a 24.3% decline. New pending listings fell from 136 to 103. New Smyrna Beach also recorded a decline of 25.3%.

Different patterns of buyer activity. This chart compares weekly new pending listings in Ormond Beach and Deltona over the past six months. While both cities experience fluctuations in weekly activity, their October year-over-year trends tell different stories. Adjust the date range or select YoY to explore how each city’s activity compares with its historical performance.

For housing professionals, the distinction matters. A regional market can be gaining momentum while individual communities within it are moving in the opposite direction.

Fall typically brings a slowdown in housing activity, but the timing and magnitude can vary significantly by market.

That’s why a change in new pending listings needs seasonal context.

Consider the average weekly new pending listings in three Volusia County cities between September and October 2026:

Historical trends add another layer. Ormond Beach also experienced an increase in average weekly new pending listings between September and October in 2025, but the increase was smaller. Deltona experienced an increase during that period in 2025 before reversing direction this year.

The lesson isn’t that one city is stronger than another. It’s that seasonal changes can play out differently even in neighboring communities.

A decline in new pending listings may be consistent with a market’s typical seasonal pattern. A smaller-than-usual decline could signal improving momentum. And an increase in activity deserves a closer look to determine whether it represents a sustained shift or a short-term fluctuation.

The Florida example raises a useful question: Are changes in buyer activity consistent across the communities you serve, or is the broader market trend masking important differences?

Four metrics can help you recognize changes, understand what they might signal and put that information to work.

What to watch: Compare new pending listings with the same period last year and track changes over several consecutive weeks. Look for activity that differs from the market’s typical seasonal pattern.

What it could mean: Sustained increases may indicate strengthening buyer activity, while declines can signal weakening demand. A smaller-than-usual seasonal decline may also be an early sign of stabilization.

How to use it: Identify communities where buyer activity is changing. Agents can use that information to set expectations with buyers and sellers, while loan officers can help referral partners understand where transaction opportunities may be improving.

What to watch: Compare the number of new listings entering the market with new pending listings. Is the gap between them widening or narrowing?

What it could mean: When new pending activity increases relative to new listings, buyers may be absorbing a greater share of incoming supply. When new listings grow faster than new pending activity, more homes may be competing for buyers’ attention.

How to use it: Evaluate whether buyers are likely to encounter more competition or more choices. For agents, that can inform pricing and offer strategies. For lending teams, it can help identify markets where purchase activity may be gaining momentum relative to available opportunities.

What to watch: Track the share of active listings with price reductions and whether that share is rising or falling over several weeks.

What it could mean: A rising share may suggest sellers are having difficulty attracting buyers at their initial asking prices. A declining share could indicate better alignment between pricing and buyer expectations, although changes in listing mix and seasonality can also influence the metric.

How to use it: Bring evidence into pricing conversations rather than relying solely on recent comparable sales. Loan officers can use the same information to help referral partners understand negotiating conditions and where pricing expectations may be changing.

What to watch: Compare median days on market with recent weeks and the same period last year. Look for whether homes are spending more or less time on the market.

What it could mean: Shorter marketing times alongside stronger new pending activity may reinforce evidence of improving demand. Longer marketing times, particularly when new pending activity is declining, may indicate that buyers are becoming more selective or that listings are taking longer to find the right price.

How to use it: Set more realistic expectations for listing timelines, buyer urgency and transaction pipelines. Brokerage and lending leaders can also use changes in market pace to inform team planning and activity forecasts.

If new pending listings are increasing, new listings are flat and days on market are declining, the combination provides stronger evidence of improving buyer activity than new pending listings alone.

But if new pending listings are rising alongside increasing price reductions and longer marketing times, the market may be experiencing a more complicated adjustment.

Consider Port Orange, Fla., where the share of listings with price reductions has declined since late September, but weekly new pending listings have fluctuated without a sustained increase. Fewer price reductions may be an encouraging sign, but they don’t necessarily indicate stronger buyer activity.

Different signals, different stories. In Port Orange, the share of listings with price reductions has fallen while weekly new pending listings have remained uneven. Adjust the date range to explore how these trends have changed over time.

Then compare neighboring communities. As the Florida example demonstrates, the same combination of metrics can tell different stories within a single metro area.

You can track these metrics and customize charts by city or ZIP code in HousingWire Intelligence.

The objective isn’t to predict the next market shift. It’s to recognize what is changing, understand what the evidence suggests and bring a more informed perspective to conversations with clients, referral partners and your team.

Logan’s national analysis suggests housing demand is finding some stability as mortgage rates ease. But the Florida data shows why that broader trend is only the starting point.

The more useful question isn’t simply whether the housing market is improving. It’s whether your market is improving, how you know and what that means for your business.

National trends provide context. Local data helps you make better decisions.

Methodology: The analysis uses HousingWire Data and covers single-family residential listings in the Deltona–Daytona Beach–Ormond Beach metro area in Volusia County, Florida.

October year-over-year comparisons use five weekly reporting periods in both 2025 and 2026. September-to-October comparisons use average weekly new pending listings to account for the different number of reporting weeks in each month.

New pending listings measure properties newly entering pending status during the reporting period, rather than the total number of properties under contract or completed sales.

All figures reflect data available through Nov. 6, 2026.

ElevenLabs Unveils Model Customization, ElevenAI and Partner Program

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

ElevenLabs announced on November 11, 2026 that it will help businesses fine-tune selected open-source language models and its voice models using data from live deployments, alongside a new ElevenAI workplace app and partner program introduced at its New York Summit.

The package extends ElevenLabs beyond voice generation and transcription into software for managing customer conversations and employee work. CEO Mati Staniszewski said conversational agents would become “critical infrastructure for every enterprise, as essential as cloud or payments” within a few years, framing the products as a broader enterprise deployment push.

ElevenLabs plans to begin the customization work in the coming months. Businesses will work with its researchers to tune models for their own customers and operating context, selecting optimization metrics such as customer satisfaction or reliability. Because the data comes from live deployments, companies can continue refining the models as interactions accumulate rather than relying only on a general industry model.

ElevenLabs already applies the industry-specific approach through Scribe v3 Medical, a healthcare transcription model built to recognize specialized terminology. Staniszewski said a domain-optimized financial-services model is also in development.

The previewed Interaction Engine addresses a different problem: deciding how a conversation should run. It manages turn-taking and interruptions and can assign different language models to different parts of an exchange, using a cheaper model for triage and a frontier model for complex tasks. The design keeps routine exchanges from requiring a frontier model at every step and is intended to limit token use.

ElevenLabs says ElevenAgents, its platform for deploying voice and chat agents, will add Memory and Routines. Memory will recognize returning authenticated customers and distill each conversation into a structured record of preferences, constraints and future occasions, while leaving the customer relationship management system as the primary customer record. A customer who prefers Spanish and can only talk after 5 p.m., for example, would not need to repeat those details on the next call.

Routines, also forthcoming, is designed to automate multi-step work before, during and after conversations, connecting with existing tools and systems. Examples include pre-screening a mortgage applicant and calling back within minutes, reminding patients about visits and offering canceled slots to waitlisted patients, emailing reports, writing to a customer relationship management system or initiating calls. Together, the features move ElevenAgents beyond answering inquiries toward retaining context and completing the work surrounding a conversation.

For employees, Staniszewski previewed ElevenAI, an app that listens to spoken conversations, including in-person meetings, and produces notes, summaries and action items. It can also provide live assistance and pass context between people and agents. ElevenLabs says the app takes meeting notes with participants’ consent and includes notice and consent mechanisms.

Free early access to ElevenAI opened with the announcement. Dictation and meeting notes use Scribe v3, which ElevenLabs describes as its recorded-audio transcription model covering 102 languages; the separate Scribe v3 Live variant is built for low-latency, real-time audio.

ElevenLabs reports more than 550 partners across 44 countries, including Google Cloud, IBM, Deloitte, DXC and Genesys. The new Partner Program lets consultancies, systems integrators, software vendors and agencies integrate ElevenAgents into enterprise technology stacks or work with ElevenLabs’ forward-deployed engineers on complex deployments. An alliance with McKinsey and QuantumBlack, AI by McKinsey, pairs the platform with McKinsey’s enterprise-transformation work.

The accompanying Marketplace initially offers a curated catalog of partner-built applications for healthcare, financial services, insurance, education and retail. Enterprises can browse the applications and contact their developers; purchasing through existing ElevenLabs commitments is planned to start in 2027.

ElevenLabs and NVIDIA announced that Nemotron 3.5 Super VL is available in ElevenAgents and that ElevenLabs plans to add 15 MW of NVIDIA Vera Rubin compute capacity on top of its existing Blackwell Ultra systems. The added capacity is intended for large deployments with security or data-residency requirements and to give ElevenLabs researchers access to optimized compute.

The expanded collaboration also covers an on-premises voice agent for Centralna e-Rejestracja, the central appointment system in Poland’s national e-Health platform, with EasyCall supporting delivery. The agent calls patients who only have landlines to remind them about appointments and help them reschedule. ElevenLabs says the central system handles 40 million appointments annually and has no-show rates of 10% to 20%; the deployment uses NVIDIA Confidential Computing to keep citizen data and audio within government infrastructure and protect them during processing.

The Summit package makes ElevenLabs’ enterprise strategy dependent on more than model performance: tuning and Memory draw on data accumulated in live use, Routines connects conversations to business systems, and the partner program and Marketplace create routes into enterprises. The central execution challenge is therefore twofold: moving the previewed and early-access products into broader production use, and progressing Marketplace browsing to purchases through existing ElevenLabs commitments.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/digital-health/maven-clinic-takes-new-dtc-vertical-network-475-commercial-plans" hreflang="en">Maven Clinic takes new DTC vertical in-network with 475+ commercial plans</a>

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →
Maven Clinic takes new DTC vertical in-network with 475+ commercial plans" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Maven Clinic, a virtual provider for women’s and family health, is making its new DTC vertical in-network with over 475 commercial health plans.

The first program to be in-network, starting now, is hormone care. Starting on Jan. 1, 2027, its GLP-1 program will also be covered. The fee-for-service contracts represent about 80% of commercially insured lives, according to the company. It is able to tap into the vast network through direct contracts and a third-party partner that it can’t name on the record. Coverage and applicable copays will vary by plan.

“We’re really doing this because of access [gaps]…the in network coverage removes that barrier,” Alex Kubal, general manager of Maven's Consumer Division, told Fierce Healthcare.

Also in 2027, Maven plans to roll out in its DTC vertical several additional specialties, including OBGYN care, maternal-fetal medicine, nurse practitioners for general women’s health and nutrition. Employees can use their health plan to get care for PMOS, endometriosis, hormonal, sexual or metabolic health, regardless of whether or not their employer sponsors a Maven program. Cash pay will also remain available.

“We need to be able to maintain pathways for both,” Kubal said. “We want people to get access to high-quality clinical care, which we see as a gap in the market.”

For example, treating perimenopause symptoms remains a challenge due to a lack of standardized diagnostics. “What we’re trying to do is really educate women and make it a safe place for women to come and talk about what they’re experiencing, and get high quality clinical care, so they don’t have to suffer through it,” Kubal said.

The DTC business, which launched six months ago, “is seeing really great traction from consumers,” per Kubal. It has grown by 55% a month, on average, in terms of subscriptions. In October, its GLP-1 program grew 80% from the prior month. While government payers are part of the discussion, going in-network with them is not on Maven's immediate roadmap, Kubal said.
